11 Performance Analysis of U-Net and Fully Convolutional Regression Network on Jetson Nano for Real-Time Inventory Analysis

Vinayak Bharadi, Suha Mukadam, Rahul Prasad, Kavyashree Upparakakula, and Janhavi Jaygade

Finolex Academy of Management and Technology, Ratnagiri, Maharashtra, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-11

11.1 Introduction: Background and Driving Forces

The application of computer vision in analyzing grocery store photographs offers valuable data, serving purposes such as ensuring accurate product display, aiding visually impaired shoppers in store navigation, and automating inventory management. However, two key challenges need to be addressed for leveraging computer vision effectively: object detection and recognition. Object detection involves identifying objects within an image, while object recognition involves identifying the specific category to which the detected object belongs [1]. These challenges can become more complex due to factors such as low-quality images, partial occlusion of objects, and the extensive variety of object types present in a grocery store setting.

Effective inventory analysis in real-time is a crucial task for businesses of all scales, enabling them to monitor inventory levels instantly, identify potential shortages, and make well-informed decisions regarding ordering and restocking. Traditional inventory analysis methods involving manual counting and paper records are prone to errors and time-consuming [2].

Computer vision and machine learning are the buzzing research domains in the recent years, mainly in the area to automate inventory analysis. This approach holds significant potential for enhancing the efficiency and accuracy of inventory management. A particularly promising platform for real-time inventory analysis is the Jetson Nano. The Jetson Nano is a compact, cost-effective, and energy-efficient computer specifically designed for machine learning applications. Its capabilities make it well-suited for rapidly and precisely detecting and identifying objects in images, thus facilitating real-time inventory analysis.

In this chapter, we introduce a real-time inventory analysis system powered by the Jetson Nano. The system utilizes object detection and analysis techniques to accurately identify products on store shelves and continuously monitor their inventory levels. Through rigorous evaluation on a dataset of grocery store shelf images, we demonstrate the system's remarkable accuracy in detecting and identifying products. Additionally, our system showcases its capability to track inventory levels in real-time and promptly detect potential stockouts. We firmly have confidence that our system can significantly enhance the efficiency and precision of inventory management practices. Consequently, we anticipate that our system will prove to be an invaluable asset for businesses seeking to optimize their inventory management processes [3].

11.2 Problem Statement

As part of their initiative to enhance operational efficiency in their large-format stores, the company is implementing aisle-scanning robots. These autonomous robots will be responsible for monitoring product availability on shelves, verifying prices, and providing precise location data for a vast inventory of over 100,000 items in each store. Moreover, the robots will gather valuable data on purchasing patterns, enabling the company to improve inventory forecasting and management. The robots employ a computer vision-based system, bolstered by a convolutional neural network, an artificial intelligence technology trained to recognize visual patterns. This system enables the robots to identify a diverse range of objects, including products, prices, and shelf labels. By implementing aisle-scanning robots, the company anticipates substantial time and cost savings. The robots will automate manual tasks such as inventory checks and price verification, allowing employees to dedicate their time to customer service and merchandising. Additionally, the data collected by the robots will contribute to ongoing operational improvements within the company.

The incorporation of aisle-scanning robots is merely a single instance of the company's commitment to leveraging technology for operational enhancements. In addition, the company is actively investing in other pioneering technologies, including AIML. By embracing these technologies, the company aims to automate various tasks, enhance overall efficiency, and provide improved customer service.

11.3 Related Work/Literature Survey

Zhu, X., Zhang, J., and Liu, Z. [4] proposed a weakly supervised framework for grocery product recognition from shelf images. The framework is based on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that is trained with a few labelled images and a large number of unlabelled images. The CNN is able to learn to recognize products from the unlabelled images by using the labelled images as a guide. The results of the paper show that the proposed framework is able to achieve high accuracy with a small number of labelled images.

In [5] Li, Y., Zhang, L., and Wang, Z. proposed a deep learning-based method for grocery product recognition in shelf images. The method uses a two-stage approach. Initially, a region proposal network (RPN) is used to generate a set of candidate regions. Later on for stage two, a classifier is used to classify each candidate region as a product or background. The results show that the proposed method has achieved high accuracy on a challenging dataset of grocery shelf images.

Chen, Y., Li, J., and Zhang, Y. [6] discussed a method for grocery product recognition that fuses data from multiple modalities, including images, text, and barcodes. The method uses a CNN to extract features from the images, a RNN to extract features from the text, and a SVM to classify the products. The proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that use only one modality.

Wang, H., Li, Y., and Wang, Y. [7] have discussed a method for grocery product recognition on shelf images that uses a hybrid attention mechanism. The attention mechanism is used to focus the attention of the model on the most important parts of the image. The results of the paper show that the proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that do not use an attention mechanism.

Zhang, Z., Liu, Y., and Wang, H. [8] proposed a method for grocery product recognition on shelf images that uses a generative adversarial network (GAN). The GAN is used to generate synthetic images of products that are similar to the real images. The real and synthetic images are then used to train a classifier. The results of the paper show that the proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that do not use a GAN.

J.M.S. do Carmo, M.R. de Souza et al. in [9] presented a vision-based line following system for mobile robots in complex environments. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a controller to steer the robot in the direction of the line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

Al-Aziz, M.A. Al-Duwaisan et al. [10] discussed a vision-based line following system for mobile robots using deep learning. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a deep learning model to classify the image as a line or not a line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

S.K. Sahoo, S.K. Panda, and B.B. Mishra [11] presented a vision-based line following system for mobile robots using reinforcement learning. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a reinforcement learning agent to learn how to follow the line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

11.4 Proposed Solution

Mobile robot navigation systems that rely on lines, structures, and signs have gained global popularity. The primary goal of such systems is to develop a mobile robot capable of utilizing lines, landmarks, or signs as reference points for navigating along predetermined paths or towards specific destinations. The central focus of these systems is typically line following, which is commonly achieved using a vision-based approach. In literature, the camera-equipped mobile robot is often referred to as an Autonomous Guided Vehicle (AGV), specifically designed for navigating within industrial or factory environments.

The AGV is trained to identify the various line shapes that could be captured by the camera and react appropriately. In fact, an obstacle avoidance algorithm has been added because accidents of this nature are very common in a factory setting. The processing time needed for this work, while extremely advanced for its time, is too long for use in many real-time applications since it can take up to 300ms to process a single image. This is primarily because of the incredibly complicated technique used and the era's hardware restrictions.

We are creating a model that can identify the object and provide us 2-dimensional (x, y) coordinates that represent a rectangle bounding box around the image. The detection of goods stocked in grocery stores is a challenge. After breaking down the problem, it is clear that the Computer Vision object detection technique may be used to solve it, enabling us to find the coordinates of the products that are clearly apparent in the photographs. In order to build a trustworthy and effective model, we'll be using the most recent object detection algorithms, which are built on top of convolutional neural nets and deep neural net layers.

11.4.1 Hardware and Software

11.4.1.1 Hardware Requirements

11.4.1.1.1 Jetson Nano Developer Kit (4 GB)

A compact, potent computer that can run many neural networks simultaneously is the Jetson Nano Developer Kit. It has four USB ports, two CSI ports, 64-bit processors, and can be connected to the internet via LAN. It can also be powered by battery or DC power supply (if powered by DC supply, a jumper must be connected). The Jetson Nano has a GPU (Graphics processing unit) that helps it process images quickly. It requires 5 watts of power to start.

11.4.1.1.2 Computer Screen

A visual display, some circuitry, a casing, and a power supply typically make up a monitor.

11.4.1.1.3 64GB SD Card

Data is stored on a Secure Digital (SD) memory card. A 90 MB per sec SD card is used. The Jetson Nano's OS is stored on an SD card. Memory is kept in the SD card.

11.4.1.1.4 Power Supply 5 V DC

To start the Jetson Nano, we need to use a 5-volt DC supply. The power supply can be either from a battery or from a power supply. The power supply needs to be converted from AC to DC 5 V.

11.4.1.1.5 Power Supply 12 V for Motors

To move the robot, we need to supply power to its motors. A power supply of 12 V DC is required. To power Jetson and other components we will require the power supply.

11.4.1.1.6 Motor

They are key components in mobilizing the robot. The speed of the motor depends on the amount of current applied. In a robot, electric motors are used to move the robot's joints and limbs. The motors are controlled by the robot's computer, which sends signals to the motors to tell them how fast and in what direction to rotate.

The type of electric motor used in a robot depends on the robot's application. For example, a robot that needs to move quickly will use a different type of motor than a robot that needs to move with precision.

11.4.1.1.7 Motor Drivers (4 Channel)

As per the output given to the drivers the mortars will move. It will convert the low voltage to high voltage which will move the robot more efficiently. The four channels are used to parallelly use four motors at a time. This will help to move it sideways, forward and backwards.

11.4.1.1.8 Arduino Uno R3

The Arduino Uno board is used here to control the motors and the path traversing. Its standard configuration is used with basic components.

11.4.1.1.9 Wi-Fi Module

It is used to provide a network to the Jetson Nano, which will help to connect to the local system.

11.4.1.1.10 Raspberry PI 5MP Camera and Logitech 720p USB Camera

This will help to take images of the surroundings and help the robot move. The robot will have two cameras, one CSI and another USB.

11.4.1.2 Software Requirements

In order to use the Jetson Nano's Arduino boards, you will need to install the following software:

Ubuntu OS - Version 18.7 for Jetson Nano

Based on Debian, Ubuntu is a free and open-source Linux distribution. It is a well-liked option for both private and business use and is renowned for its dependability, security, and use. To install Ubuntu, you can download the ISO file from the Ubuntu website and create a bootable USB drive or DVD.

Once you have created a bootable media, you can boot your computer from it and follow the on-screen instructions to install Ubuntu.

This is installed on Jetson Nano as a Jetpack. Jetpack includes a variety of features that make it a powerful development platform for the Jetson Nano.

  • CUDA and TensorRT are included for accelerated computing.
  • OpenCV and GStreamer are included for image processing and video streaming.
  • Qt and Python are included for application development.
  • A wide range of pre-built applications are included for a variety of purposes, such as AI, robotics, and machine learning.

Arduino IDE 2.0

It is a powerful tool that allows users to create a wide variety of projects.

To install the Arduino IDE, you can download it from the Arduino website.

Once you have downloaded the Arduino IDE, you can install it by following the on-screen instructions.

11.4.2 Implementation

We have built a comprehensive database of images featuring shelves and products, which includes a wide range of properties. To ensure accuracy and account for variations, we visited approximately 40 grocery stores, specifically examining lighting conditions and shelf designs. In order to assess the impact of photo quality, we captured images using four different cameras. In total, we took 354 pictures of the shelves, covering varying numbers of shelves in each image to test the effectiveness of shelf segmentation and capture images from different distances. Additionally, we systematically moved the frame down one shelf at a time to capture multiple photographs of specific rack shelves.

As a result of our efforts, we have enabled future research in image stitching, allowing the merging of multiple images depicting smaller shelves to create a comprehensive representation of the entire rack. These findings are thoroughly documented in our annotations. For reference, Figure 11.1. illustrates a collection of pictures showcasing a similar rack.

We found almost 13,000 goods spread over the 354 photos. Annotating each image took about 1.5 minutes. Notably, we recorded the pertinent data and divided the products into ten separate brand classes. The photos typically included 200 items from each class.

11.4.2.1 Mobile Robot Mobile Controller

The robot has a Raspberry Pi 5MP camera on the front that records videos in BGR format with a resolution of 1366×768 at 15 frames per second. The videos are sent to a local device for processing and then sent to a cloud-based server via a Wi-Fi module. Two DC motors on the back and two omni wheels on the front make up the differential drive system that powers the robot's movement. A Raspberry Pi camera and a Wi-Fi data collecting module make up the navigation system, which provides the input needed for image processing. Python is used on a Jetson Nano to process the images. The motor driver uses the output of the image processing to supply each of the DC motors with the appropriate voltages. The flowchart is shown in Figure 11.2.

Prior to programming, it is necessary to configure the Jetson Nano's camera and interface device for OpenCV and Python 3. Once this setup is complete, real-time video can be captured frame-by-frame. Each frame is represented as a set of three matrices, corresponding to the red, green, and blue components of the image. Subsequently, the image frame can be converted from the BGR format to the HSV format. HSV denotes hue, saturation, and value, where hue represents the color of the pixel, saturation signifies the intensity of the color, and value indicates the brightness of the pixel. With the image in HSV format, it becomes possible to define the area for each color and generate a mask. A mask is a binary image, consisting of black and white pixels, which facilitates the identification of specific objects or regions within a color image.

To remove noise from the image, a morphological transformation called dilation can be used. Dilation is a process that expands the boundaries of objects in an image. This helps to remove small, insignificant objects from the image and improve the overall quality of the image. Once the noise has been removed, features can be extracted from the input frame and a mask can be created. The features that are extracted from the image can be used to identify specific objects or areas in the image.

Figure 11.3 illustrates the process described. In this scenario, the image features that are extracted are the white lines against a dark surface. The clear contrast between the line and the background simplifies the line identification. After extracting the relevant information regarding the white line route from the image, it can be translated into the intended orientation or flight path for the mobile robot. This parameter dictates whether the robot moves straight, left, or right.

11.4.2.2 Object Detection

The first step of our pipeline is to obtain a set of bounding boxes from a query image of various items displayed on a store shelf. These bounding boxes will be used as region suggestions in the subsequent recognition stage. Each bounding box should ideally only contain one product, fit the visible package tightly, and provide a confidence score that indicates how much the detection can be trusted.

Instead of conducting a logo search on each individual shelf image, our approach involves initially extracting the structure of the store shelves before applying object recognition. In this stage, we adopt a top-down methodology instead of a bottom-up one. This approach leverages the inherent shape similarity among product classes. While tobacco products present an exception due to the standardized warning images across brands, there are often noticeable similarities within other classes. For instance, different beverage brands frequently share similar bottle structures. We assume that the products will exhibit minimal out-of-plane rotation and possess comparable aspect ratios to those commonly found on grocery store shelves.

One highly effective real-time object detection algorithm that we employ is YOLO-v5 (You Only Look Once). It utilizes a sophisticated convolutional neural network (CNN) for object detection tasks. YOLO is renowned for its exceptional speed and accuracy. Being a single-stage detector, it can predict bounding boxes and class probabilities for all objects in an image in a single pass. This grants YOLO a significant advantage in terms of speed compared to two-stage detectors like R-CNN and Faster R-CNN, which involve region proposal generation and subsequent classification as separate steps.

Here are some of the reasons YOLO is often preferred over the CNN:

  1. Speed: YOLO is much faster than other object detection algorithms, making it ideal for real-time applications.
  2. Accuracy: YOLO is still very accurate, even though it is much faster than other algorithms.
  3. Ease of use: YOLO is relatively easy to use and implement, making it a good choice for beginners.
  4. Flexibility: YOLO can be used to detect a wide variety of objects, making it a versatile tool.

The image is first divided into areas by the algorithm. Then, probabilities and bounding boxes are computed for each region. Based on the predicted probability, these bounding boxes have been given weights. The phrase “only look at the image once” refers to the fact that the algorithm only needs to traverse CNN once in order to provide predictions. The algorithm outputs the known items and their bounding boxes after non-maximal suppression. This guarantees that each object is only detected once by the object detection algorithm. In Figure 11.4, the flow is depicted.

Before recognizing products, we can first extract the structure of the supermarket shelves rather than searching for a brand label on every shelf image. As contrast to a bottom-up strategy, which would entail looking for logos on each shelf image, this is referred to as a top-down approach. In this instance, we benefit from the shape similarity between product classes. For instance, the bottle designs of many beverage companies are frequently similar. We also anticipate that there won't be any out-of-plane rotation and that the products‘ aspect ratios will be comparable to those on grocery store shelves.

In this instance, we benefit from the shape similarity between product groups. While all product categories have numerous similarities, grocery items are special in that they all have the same warning graphics across companies. For instance, the bottle shapes of several beverage brands are similar. We anticipate that there will be little out-of-plane rotation of the products and that their percentage will be similar to that of supermarket shelves. Because of this similarity, YOLOv5 is employed.

We have considered the possibility of implementing our own customized CNN. We believe that it would be possible to create a CNN that is tailored to our specific needs and that would be less cumbersome than the pre-trained models that we are currently using. One of the benefits of implementing our own CNN is that we would have more control over the architecture of the model. This would allow us to choose the specific layers and parameters that we believe would be most effective for our task. We could also experiment with different architectures to see what works best.

However, there are also some challenges associated with implementing our own CNN. One challenge is that it would be more time-consuming and difficult than using a pre-trained model. We would need to spend time designing the architecture of the model, collecting and preparing the data, and training the model. Another challenge is that it is possible that our custom CNN would not perform as well as a pre-trained model. This is because pre-trained models have been trained on large datasets of images, which gives them a significant advantage.

Overall, we believe that the benefits of implementing our own CNN outweigh the challenges. We are confident that we could create a CNN that is tailored to our specific needs and that would be less cumbersome than the pre-trained models that we are currently using. We are currently in the process of researching the different architectures that are available and also collecting data to train our custom CNN. We are hopeful that we will be able to implement our own CNN in the future work.

As previously stated, Ultralytics-compatible YOLOv5 is built on the PyTorch framework, a widely used open-source machine learning library within the AI community. It is recognized for its popularity and versatility. However, as this is a prototype design, researchers have the flexibility to customize it for specific problem domains. Modifications can be made by adding or removing layers, integrating novel image processing techniques, altering optimization methods or activation functions, and more, in order to achieve satisfactory results tailored to their specific requirements.

The information box in YOLO is displayed using the format [class, center, width, height]. From the minimal point (Xmin), the center point (Xcenter, R) can be computed as follows: Xcenter=Xmin width2Ycenter=Ycenterheight2. Because it is difficult to identify a single object, we have not given the bounding boxes names. Bounding boxes must be specified using class names, though, because YOLO needs a label parameter. Since the class numbers are zero-indexed (beginning at 0), the mark “0” represents the head of wheat since all bounding boxes are in charge of recognizing wheat. A value between 0 and 1 must be entered for the box's coordinates. As a result, the widths of Xcenter are split by the image's width, while the widths of Ycenter are divided by the image's height.

The bounding box's width, height, class, X-center, and unique picture ID now correspond to each data column. Objects can be seen in positive photos, but not in negative ones. Negative photos must be accompanied by text files in order for the YOLOv5 model to access and utilize them during training. Due to the absence of objects, these photos lack bounding boxes.

To easily place the product's boundary boxes on the shelves, use the product identification module. On compact devices like smartphones, this module can be run in real time. Finding the brand logo for each product is the next stage. In addition to the segmentation of product and non-product images, we also propose a method for identifying shelf boundaries. Although not required, this is a smart idea.

We can compute and estimate the number of shelves once we have established the limits of the shelves. We can compute the products’ Y-axis projection histogram in order to assess the distribution of the items on the shelves. We can apply a Gaussian filter to the signal to exclude noise from false positive signals. This signal's vertices and center points are automatically identified as product positions and shelf limits, respectively.

Regarding the automatic robot traversing, the step is currently implemented by putting a white strip as a path for the robot and the camera mounted on the lower part will be segmenting the path from the surrounding as shown in Figure 11.3. In future we plan for use of Lidar sensors mounted on top to gather the environmental knowledge, currently this is beyond the computational capacity of our system hence path traversing is used.

The prototype here is tested with people around, as it is using a white line for getting towards the selves, if there are people around their presence is not hindering the performance of the robot.

11.5 Results and Discussions

Computer vision techniques have focused on counting and localizing objects in images. Object counting and localization are essential steps for quantitative analysis in large-scale microscopy applications. This process is made more challenging by target objects that overlap, are closely grouped, or have fuzzy boundaries. Earlier deep learning-based approaches for building density maps achieved a high degree of accuracy for object counting by assuming that the integration of the density map is equivalent to object counting. However, this paradigm is not useful for accurate localization when objects show significant overlap. In deep learning, convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are commonly used to first identify objects before counting them. For example, the GCNet model first identifies objects using a CNN, then groups them using a clustering algorithm, and finally counts each instance. Although this approach is effective, it requires bounding box annotations, which can be difficult to obtain. Alternative techniques address this issue by using point-like annotations of object locations, which are much easier to collect. To implicitly count objects, the basic idea is to determine a density map. Making training samples is the initial stage in producing a density map for each image. Applying a convolution with a Gaussian kernel can produce a density map. After being normalized, the density map can be integrated to get the number of objects.

In order to detect how many objects are in an image, it is currently desired to train a fully convolutional network to transforms an image into a density map. So far, we have considered two FCN designs: U-Net and Fully Convolutional Regression Network (FCRN). Both U-Net and FCRN-A use a fixed filter size of 3×3 and three convolutional blocks—three up sampling and three down sampling. By default, there are two convolutional layers in each block for U-Net and FCRN-A, respectively. To compensate for the loss of higher quality data caused by pooling in FCRN-A, we increase this number with each new layer. In U-Net, we keep the number of filters consistent across all convolutional layers. (This is not passed directly in the case of U-Net). Further, a 3D mapping of surroundings is also implemented using Lidar on Jetson Nano; this is an addition to the path tracking, the output is shown in Fig. 19.5. The path and surrounding information will be used to drive the robot through the shelves. The results for object segmentation and detections are discussed below.

All products are detected correctly in Step A, and the system finds zero false positives and does not miss any true positives. An example is visible in Figure 11.5.

19.5.2 Step B

19.5.3 Step C

Statistical data have been presented and rearranged in above tables with seven columns:

  • Model N°: ID associated with the model image;
  • Name: textual identifier associated with the model;
  • Counter Models: number of models associated with the specific row;
  • Precision (%): Precision expressed as a percentage;
  • Recall (%): Recall expressed as a percentage;
  • F1(%): harmonic mean between Precision and Recall, expressed in percentage;
  • %: the ratio between Counter Models, and the total number of models, expressed as a percentage

8.33

There are a few ways to solve the problem of multiple objects stacked in front of the one you are observing. One way is to use a technique called depth estimation. Depth estimation is the process of determining the distance between an object and the camera. By estimating the depth of the objects in an image, we can determine which objects are stacked in front of others.

Using a method known as semantic segmentation is another option to resolve this issue. The method of semantic segmentation entails giving each pixel in a picture a label. By semantic segmentation, we can determine the different objects in an image and their boundaries. This information can then be used to determine which objects are stacked in front of others.

In addition to depth estimation and semantic segmentation, there are other techniques that can be used to solve the problem of multiple objects stacked in front of the one you are observing. These techniques include:

  1. Feature matching: This technique compares features between different images to determine which objects are in the same location.
  2. Object tracking: This technique tracks the movement of objects over time to determine their depth and position.
  3. Multi-view geometry: This technique uses multiple views of an object to determine its depth and position.

We believe that it is possible to train a system to measure the depth of objects and thus estimate what is left. This could be done by using a combination of depth estimation, semantic segmentation, and other techniques. By training a system on a large dataset of images, it would be able to learn to identify different objects and their depth. This information could then be used to estimate what is left behind objects that are stacked in front of them.

The work's future scope is as follows. To teach a system to gauge an object's depth and determine how much is left, the following issues would need to be resolved:

  1. The system would need to be able to handle occlusions. This means that it would need to be able to identify objects that are partially hidden by other objects.
  2. The system would need to be able to handle variations in lighting. This means that it would need to be able to estimate the depth of objects even if the lighting conditions change.
  3. The system would need to be able to handle a wide variety of objects. This means that it would need to be trained on a large dataset of images that include a variety of objects.

We believe that these challenges are surmountable. With enough data and training, it is possible to develop a system that can accurately measure the depth of objects and estimate what is left behind them. This would be a valuable tool for applications such as robotics, augmented reality, and self-driving cars.

11.6 Conclusion

This research presents a novel approach for identifying retail products on grocery store shelves. A newly introduced dataset facilitates the exploration of various aspects of shelf images. Our method initially segments the image to determine product locations, followed by logo classification into predefined brands. The effectiveness of our method is quantitatively evaluated on real environment images. The proposed framework comprises two distinct modules, each demonstrating commendable individual performance. To achieve a functional prototype and comprehensive performance evaluation, future work should address the challenge of handling products from unspecified categories. Additionally, this study did not employ preprocessing techniques like perspective correction or noise reduction, which could be implemented in the future to enhance system performance.

Reliably detecting stock levels on shelves remains a challenge in robotic stock assessment. The availability of low-cost visual sensors has inspired researchers to develop methods utilizing statistics and image processing techniques to extract meaningful information from images. Integration of Lidar and Motor drivers for automated movement of counting robots between shelves is a potential avenue for future exploration.

  11. Sahoo, S.K., Panda, S.K., and Mishra, B.B. A vision-based line following system for mobile robots using reinforcement learning. IEEE Access 8 (2020): 107236–107245.

12 Clinical Decision Support System for Prevention of Puberty Disorders and Fertility Issues due to Noyyal River Pollution Using Ensemble Learning Techniques

Anitha Shanmugam

Kongu Engineering College, Perundurai, Tamil Nadu, India

Chandrasekaran Viswanathan

Velalar College of Engineering and Technology, Erode, Tamil Nadu, India

Pradhanika Mohankumar

Kongu Engineering College, Perundurai, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-12

12.1 Introduction

Early puberty is linked to an increased risk of breast cancer, necessitating prompt diagnosis and effective treatment. The GnRHa stimulation test is not used in the traditional statistical methods, which rely solely on baseline characteristics to predict CPP. No machine learning-based model has been able to diagnose CPP using a single-sample 30-min post-stimulation as of yet. Features are produced from laboratory data rather than extracting them in order to increase the rank of feature importance. Breast cancer risk increases with early puberty and is accompanied by anomalies in the female reproductive system. Phthalates have an adverse effect on fertility through interfering with steroidogenesis, oocyte maturation, folliculogenesis, and embryonic development, but there are few studies in humans to support this.

Women with infertility (Lin et al. 2023) history whose pregnancies followed In Vitro Fertilisation (IVF) had considerably lower urine Di-2-Ethylhexyl Phthalate (DEHP) molar sums than those whose pregnancies did not follow IVF. Mono-2-Ethylhexyl Phthalate (MEHP) dose-dependently inhibits the expression of aromatase and the synthesis of E2 at various doses in the presence of FSH. However, the effect of phthalates on the follicular and oocyte development in humans must be considered. When compared to people who are not exposed to PCOS, humans with PCOS have lower urine amounts of phthalate metabolites. Increased levels of serum pefluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) in women are associated with higher incidences of subfecundity and prolonged gestational duration. Additionally, menstrual cycle irregularities have risen in frequency with greatest PFOA doses.

The possible impacts of water pollutants like bisphenol A (BPA), phthalates, and perfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) on female reproductive systems in humans are shown in Figure 12.1. These consequences include puberty, the onset of PCOS, infertility, ovarian function, endometriosis, and recurrent miscarriages. Additionally, the figure shows the potential implications of these compounds and the need for increased public and clinical knowledge.

These compounds may have negative effects on hormone production and/or function that may be passed down to future generations. So, with the right information, people can decide to restrict their exposure, so reducing the risk to future generations.

12.2 Review of Existing Knowledge Highlighting Research Gap

12.2.1 International Status

Using well-known machine learning methods like XGBoost and Random Forest Classifiers, Pan et al. (2019) created a prediction model to diagnose CPP, particularly in girls. Because the proposed model serves as a pre-screening tool utilized before the GnRHa test, it lowers the cost of the GnRHa-stimulation test. Canipari et al. (2020) talked about how environmental pollution affects female fertility. However, the study does not offer a way to solve the issues. Seppä et al. (2021) provided diagnosis and treatment of the main causes of infertility (Raszewski et al. 2022). The study is only concerned with treatable disorders Silva et al. (2023). Huynh et al. (2022) proposed diagnosis paradigm for puberty disorders but due to a lack of evidence, reproductive issues in Jain and Singh (2023) are unreliable and also different stages of disease are not identified.

12.2.2 National Status

Prior to in vitro fertilisation, Goyal et al. (2020) suggested machine learning techniques to forecast the likelihood of live births. However, the data is restricted to a specific area, and numerous significant elements are not taken into account. In a 2017 study, Surana et al. investigated the impact of obesity on the timing of puberty in Indian boys, as well as the relationship between gonadotropin levels and puberty. They measured serum LH, FSH and testosterone in addition to using anthropometric measurements of height and weight. Finally, they projected the serum LH and testosterone cutoff levels for the commencement of pubertal development in Indian boys. Anna Merklinger-Gruchala et al. (2017) examined air pollutants to gauge their impact during the menstrual cycle. The findings have demonstrated that the toxicants in combination with fossil fuels have an impact on the length of the luteal phase. In a retrospective study on delayed puberty in northern India, Bhakhri et al. (2010) found that chronic systemic illness is the main contributor to the delay. They suggested that puberty delays could be avoided by early detection and treatment. Bajpai & Menon (2011) identified current problems with Precocious Puberty (PP) and proposed that GnRHa test may quickly and accurately identify precocious condition. With an Indian perspective, Kumar et al. (2015) offered a straightforward method to treat gonadotrophin dependent/CPP and indicated that the PP problem needs to be identified at an early stage and treated appropriately. A review of all research on puberty problems, its diagnostic subtypes, and the evaluation of early and normal puberty is given by Brämswig & Dübbers (2009). The author came to the conclusion that puberty abnormalities should be correctly identified, accurately diagnosed, and treated by a paediatric endocrinologist. Witchel and Plant (2020) gave a summary of the illnesses associated with puberty as well as its process. Different approaches to the diagnosis and treatment of puberty disorders have been proposed by David et al. (2017). By examining the physical characteristics and family histories of both boys and girls, they were able to pinpoint the causes of both premature and delayed puberty. In order to test for puberty abnormalities, the author additionally utilized few parameters of serum FSH, LH, testosterone (for males) or E2 (for girls), and bone age radiography.

12.3 Research Gap

According to the studies already in existence, several investigations have been made into various methods of diagnosis and potential root causes of puberty disorders. Additionally, the majority of studies have been conducted to determine potential causes, treatments, and assessments for puberty problems. According to these studies, changes in eating habits and environmental conditions have resulted in a sharp rise in puberty disorders and infertility. The main causes, according to studies, include high levels of obesity, stress, city lifestyle, and hormonal changes. Both male and female reproductive are impacted by water and air pollution, particularly in terms of puberty, PCOD, ovarian function, endometriosis, and miscarriage. However, no reliable research has been done to pinpoint the high-risk contaminant.

With the evolution of technology and the environment, it is essential to manage the vast amounts of data in an accurate way. Machine learning algorithms have only been used by a very small number of academics to identify high-risk factors for puberty problems. However, in order to solve this problem, data analytics must be combined with one of the cutting-edge ensemble learning approaches. Additionally, a precise pre-screening tool must be created in order to lower the cost of the GnRHa test procedure so that low-income individuals can utilise it. Since the number of people impacted by these issues is increasing exponentially, there is an urgent need for solutions in Tamil Nadu's western districts.

12.3.1 Reasons for the Problem

The prevalence of infertility issues and disorders of puberty rises daily, not just in a specific region but also in many states. In addition to other factors, environmental contamination is the primary cause of these issues. Therefore, infertility issues must be addressed well before puberty. Precocious or early puberty and delayed puberty, which has a greater impact on fertility, are the two categories of puberty disorders. There is an urgent need to address these issues in order to preserve public health and pass forward healthy generations.

The following are highly likely to occur in the Kongu belt (Coimbatore, Tiruppur, Erode, and Salem):

  • More cases of cancer among people
  • More childless and infertile couples
  • More livestock dying from illnesses brought on by river water
  • Due to polluted water, more farmers are being affected

Smart cities provide services that are typically based on data extraction, which helps to improve people's quality of life. Public administration makes use of these smart city data to improve efficiency, cut costs, and offer new services. However, the data collected at smart city data centres is not always precise and controllable using the current methods. Designing a smart gadget to diagnose puberty abnormalities which result in infertility made more difficult by the restricted resources and varied traits.

12.3.2 Solutions to the Problems

  • The creation of prediction models that can be used to prescreen patients and aid in the diagnosis of CPP
  • Using ensemble learning techniques, identifying the high-risk factors for puberty problems and their effects on infertility
  • Using the Pearson Correlation Test, conducting a correlation analysis between characteristics and menstrual cycle length
  • Executing a model comparison with characteristics from other puberty issue areas
  • Prescriptive analytical techniques recommends the best treatments for issues with puberty and infertility
  • Early/late puberty disorders can be predicted by taking wise policy decisions addressing environmental, psychological, and physical aspects

12.4 Goals of the Proposed Work

12.4.1 Objectives of the Study

  • To evaluate how menstrual cycle and its phases length are affected by water pollutants
  • To collect various CPP-related variables and predict output from the GnRHa-stimulation test using machine learning models
  • To find the high risk factors of puberty problems and their effects on infertility by using ensemble learning techniques
  • To make policy decisions involving physiologic, psychological, and environmental components will help forecast early/late puberty issues
  • To compare the model with features from other areas of puberty difficulties

12.4.2 Specific Aims of the Study

  • To create diagnostic model and collect test sample 30 minutes after stimulation in order to detect CPP and lessen the anxiety that children experience as a result of having several blood samples taken
  • To take into account additional elements such as growth rate, race/ethnicity, medical imaging, change in cycle duration, and age range of people
  • To distinguish between PP that is progressive from PP that is not progressive to prevent unnecessary treatment
  • To enlarge the data sample in light of the likelihood that CPP cases may result in a higher incidence rate
  • To raise the level of model automation by strengthening the algorithm that matches the manually examined data
  • To generate features from laboratory data rather than extracting them from free text, in order to increase the rank of feature importance
  • To improve the accuracy of unstructured features

12.5 Relevance of the Proposed Study

12.5.1 Relevance to Public Health

  • Highly polluted Coimbatore, Tiruppur, Erode, and Salem regions of Tamil Nadu are having an impact on people's ability for reproduction
  • The prevalence of infertility is not just among those who live near rivers in Tamil Nadu's western districts, but also in other areas
  • There are many causes of low sperm counts, and one of them is environmental pollution
  • Residents of the Noyyal river in Tamil Nadu encounter a similar issue to those in Uttar Pradesh
  • Water and air pollution, particularly in Tamil Nadu's western districts, play a significant role in these issues

12.5.2 Relative Importance in a Given Area

  • Numerous dye and bleaching industries are reportedly discharging their wastewater into Tamil Nadu's Noyyal river, according to various media sources
  • As a result, the river has grown extremely polluted and is having an impact on both humans and the environment
  • The river water is plainly rendering people sterile

12.6 Applicability

12.6.1 Short Term

  • Determining the underlying organic cause of gonadotropin-dependent precocious puberty requires evaluation
  • The impact of various chemicals (Ghosh et al. 2022) on polluted water on female reproduction will be studied
  • Comparing various models on predicting the likelihood of a live birth after the completion of an IVF cycle
  • Using computational and predictive models based on machine learning to provide a clinical decision support tool for physicians and patients, which will increase the success rates of assisted reproductive technology.

12.6.2 Long Term

  • The elimination of reproductive issues and disorders of puberty transported by widespread water pollution
  • Raising clinician and public awareness of some of the substances, which could have an impact on future generations
  • Governmental leadership, environmental education, and social action are necessary for effective protection against chemical exposures
  • Proper chemical education can aid people in reducing their exposure to these substances in food and water, thus reducing the threat to upcoming generations
  • Patients employing computational and prediction models based on machine learning will benefit from advancements in assisted reproductive technology success rates

12.7 Methodology

The proposed model uses data gathered from patients in lab centers who had undergone the GnRH test. Then the factors are predicted using ensemble learning techniques like Bagging (Bootstrap aggregation), random splits, and k-fold cross-validation to produce a prescriptive solution, either regarding the environment or good habits.

The prediction model was developed using clinical data, measurements of bone age, and blood tests performed 30 minutes after stimulation. The early emergence of secondary sex characteristics are characteristics of CPP, which is brought on by the early activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. Different machine learning and ensemble methods are used to create the candidate model.

To categorize as CPP or non-CPP at various time periods, the model's performance is assessed using accuracy, high speed, simplicity, sensitivity, and specificity metrics. The GnRHa test has restricted time periods since it is expensive, especially in places where healthcare is not provided for free. Consideration is given to the model which shortens the GnRHa test following its injection.

12.7.1 Participants and Study Design

Precocious puberty (PP) is defined as the onset of menstruation before the age of nine in girls who have at least one sign of pubertal development growth. All girls with PP will undergo a GnRHa stimulation test to determine CPP, and any females with congenital adrenal hyperplasia or hypothyroidism will be eliminated.

The study population is considered for children in the age group of 8–16 years for puberty disorders and women in the age group of 17–32 years for infertility problems. In the selected area (Erode), all taluks will form the sampling frame. One taluk from the district will be selected by Random sampling and segregated as rural and urban areas.

12.7.2 Menstrual Cycle Characteristics

The number of days between the start of one cycle and the next was used to calculate the overall cycle duration. Each woman provided daily morning saliva samples for the duration of one menstrual cycle to measure progesterone and 17-E2 levels (P). Participants are responsible for collecting their own saliva samples by putting sugarless, preservative-treated chewing gum into plastic tubes to induce salivation (sodium azide). While the monitored menstrual cycle is occurring, samples are kept at room temperature. An I-125-based radioimmunoassay kit is used to determine the E2 and P concentrations in saliva samples from each cycle. After the assays were finished, the daily E2 concentrations for each cycle were analyzed to determine the day with the biggest midcycle decline in E2 levels, which corresponds to the day of ovulation. The number of days between the start of menstruation and the midcycle E2 drop day was used to determine how long the follicular phase lasted. The duration of the luteal phase is the difference between the midcycle E2 drop day and the first day of the consequent self-reported menses.

Two measures of body weight were taken: once before and after the monitored menstrual cycle. A questionnaire was used to gather data on the subjects‘ birth date, birth weight and length, years of education, number of children, age of menarche, and average duration of cycles, hormone medications, smoking status, and daily cigarette consumption. The birth weight divided by the birth length, given in kilogrammes per square metre, is known as the ponderal index, a measure of fatness at birth that has been linked to the ability to predict menstrual cycle characteristics.

12.7.3 Data Preprocessing

Data collection can be done with different categories such as based on area (urban/rural), age group, and causes. The samples mean values were grouped by age and used to replace any missing values for continuous variables. Menarchal experience is one that discrete variable has a value of 0. The least degree of filling is used for discrete variables in the Tanner stage for the breast and pubic region (stage 1).

12.7.4 Feature Importance

The contribution to prediction can be represented by feature importance, which is a feature weight. The number of times a feature has been chosen as a tree node is added up to determine its relevance in XGBoost. In random forest, the out-of-bag (OOB) error is used to determine the relevance of each feature. In the relevant bootstrap sample, the average prediction error for training observations is known as OOB error. After randomly adding noise perturbations to OOB data, a feature with larger OOB error difference is more relevant. For each feature, the total of the feature significance values is calculated to get a rank.

12.7.5 GnRHa Stimulation Test and CPP Diagnosis

Throughout the GnRHa stimulation test, blood samples are obtained for LH and FSH measurements at the basal time point (zero), 30, 60, and 120 minutes. Girls with the following characteristics were diagnosed with CPP: Following a GnRHa stimulation test, there are three things that are present: (a) secondary sexual characteristics, such as pubic hair and/or the development of breasts; (b) peak LH levels of 5 IU/L and a ratio of peak LH to FSH value of 0.6 and (c) Girls were classified as non-CPP cases if they did not display secondary sexual features by the time they were 8 years old and did not show negative reactions to the GnRHa stimulation test.

According to the Tanner stage, reports from parents and caregivers as well as examinations by paediatric endocrinologists were used to determine whether secondary sexual characteristics including menstruation/menarche, pubic hair growth, and breast development were present. The body mass index (BMI) is calculated using the height and weight of the participant. Body weight, height, and BMI were transformed into Z-scores for children aged 5 to 19 years because BMI fluctuates with age and sex in developing children, and this helps to compare weight among different populations to define obesity.

Obesity was defined as a BMI +2 standard deviations (SDs), which is equivalent to a BMI of 30 kg/m2 at age 19 (BMI Z-score). Bone age was determined using left-hand radiography, and the bone age-to-chronological age discrepancy (BA-CA) was estimated as bone age chronological age (years). Basal blood E2, LH, and FSH levels, prolactin, testosterone and basal E2 were measured in the laboratory.

12.7.6 Model Development Based on Machine Learning Algorithms: Training and Validation

Tree learning classifiers offer strong interpretability and support nonlinear interactions between features. The models are created using extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost) and random forest, two tree-based ensemble binary classification methods. For comparing the performance of ensemble and nonensemble models, decision trees and linear supported vector machines (SVM) are also utilised in the classification process. A scalable tree boosting and efficient learning algorithm designed specifically for medical research is called XGBoost. To reduce errors generated by current models, it trains a series of models. Decision trees serve as models in a gradient boosting tree model, XGBoost. Normalization is not necessary because XGBoost is not based on distance.

Another well-known ensemble learning approach, random forest combines many different trees to train decision trees concurrently using replacement data. Tree learners are subjected to bootstrap aggregating, which improves performance as variance goes down. Random forest can handle nonlinear data and is noise-resistant. SVM is a maximum margin hyperplane binary classifier. A classification model that resembles a tree is called a decision tree classifier. Monte Carlo and k-fold cross-validations for parameter tuning are used to get reliable results and avoid overfitting. Five subsets comprise the training set, of which four are used for training and one for testing. Grid search is used for parameter adjustment in the inner cross-validation. The best performance metric on the inner test set is found for the model fitted on the training set. Age (years), body weight (Z-score), height (Z-score), BMI (Z-score), obesity (yes/no), pubic hair (Tanner stages 1 to 5), menstrual/menarche (yes/no), BA-CA (years), uterine development, follicle presence, uterine volume, left and right ovary volumes, and bone age were the variables that were taken from the participants’ clinical records.

Basal E2 levels and LH and FSH levels 30 minutes after the stimulation test made up the remaining factors. To determine how well the suggested system's results will generalise to CPP and non-CPP subjects, cross-validation is employed. The model's performance is contrasted with models that were developed using LH and FSH measurements acquired at various time intervals and applied to internal and external validation data. Figure 12.2 depicts the steps in the proposed work.

12.7.7 Model Interpretation

The local interpretable model-agnostic explanations (LIME) algorithm is used to interpret feature contributions for each prediction, overcoming the drawbacks of the black-box machine learning model and giving physicians more knowledge about the prediction model in clinical practise. The likelihood score and the cutoff for the model's prediction were disclosed via LIME analysis. The maximum number of features that can be explained, the number of neighbourhood samples that should be generated, and the machine learning prediction function are the major parameters of LIME.

12.7.8 Statistical Analysis

To determine whether continuous factors related with menstrual cycle length, Pearson's correlation test was employed. Using one-way analyses of variance (ANOVA), the relationship between categorical variable and cycle length was investigated. Before being used in the analyses or the models, non-normally distributed variables underwent log transformation. Principal Component Analysis (PCA), a statistical approach that converts a set of interrelated original variables into a set of uncorrelated new variables, is used to evaluate the combined impact of water contaminants. To better understand the impact of each original variable in the Principal Components, Varimax rotation is employed to extract each variable's contribution to a particular PC.

Using simple and multivariate linear regression models, the second step of the primary analyses examines the impact of each PC on the length of before and after the menstrual cycle. To evaluate the impact of joint pollutant exposure on the length of the follicular and luteal phases of the menstrual cycle separately, the same technique is used. The mean, standard deviation, and percentages are used to present data for continuous and categorical variables. Continuous variables are studied using the independent Student's t-test, whereas categorical variables are analysed using Pearson's 2 test. Python is used for all calculations and analysis, together with modules like Scikit-learn, Pandas, Lime, and Matplotlib. In order to identify the best set of features, the correlation algorithm is assessed.

12.8 Sequence of Flow in the Proposed Work

  • Evaluation of how water pollution affects different phases of the menstrual cycle
  • Preprocessing and organizing data relevant to the investigation
  • Creation of a novel regression model for oocyte number prediction
  • Building a machine learning model to forecast the GnRHa-stimulation test response
  • Creation of a prediction model to detect CPP
  • Determining connections between the input characteristics and the GnRHa test outcomes
  • After data rearranging, randomly separating the features
  • Parameter tuning is used to address the overfitting issue
  • k-fold validation is carried out, and the best features are chosen
  • Identification of puberty disorders‘ high risk factors and their effects on infertility
  • Utilizing the Pearson Correlation Test and conducting a correlation analysis between characteristics and menstrual cycle length
  • Comparing the model with characteristics from related puberty issues
  • Application of prescriptive analytical techniques to recommend the best possible treatment options
  • Deciding clinically on early or late puberty problems that cause infertility

12.9 Significance/Outcome of the Study

  • A business process map of the facilities and systems in place that provide healthcare services for patients with puberty abnormalities and infertility issues
  • A literature assessment of published research that consider the efficacy of the various components of adult and maternity healthcare
  • A comparison of the efficacy of several therapy modalities for treating puberty disorders and reproductive issues, along with a scenario analysis
  • The government makes decisions for public health by eliminating the causes of infertility issues and disorders of puberty

12.10 Conclusion

The proposed method will be used for developing methods to identify the stage in which the patient belongs among the various stages of puberty and infertility problems. Machine learning with ensemble averaging techniques have demonstrated their effectiveness in a wide range of applications, particularly those that call for analysis of physiological, psychological, and environmental factors susceptible to water pollutants. For developers, these applications present new difficulties. It is necessary to create a new class of quick, dependable techniques in order to ensure an acceptable degree of quality for the delivery of events.

In order to achieve natural generation growth, this research will examine how to apply the knowledge that was retrieved to improve the operational effectiveness of the system that controls puberty. Due to the enormous amount of data being produced, it is essential to utilize the right measure of interestingness in order to identify behavioural patterns that strongly correlate with data. The suggested behavioural patterns capture both temporal correlations associated with such co-occurrences in the data as well as association-like co-occurrences.

Despite the fact that real-time applications require models linked to data patterns, no such method has yet to be developed. The development of an application or other technology to assess a patient's disease and examine its underlying causes is envisaged for the future. Keeping in mind the current resources in India, to streamline the algorithm for precocious puberty (PP) diagnosis and treatment, particularly gonadotrophin dependent PP.

13 Obesity Prediction Using Machine Learning

Kala Raja Mohan, Nagadevi Bala Nagaram, R. Narmada Devi, and Regan Murugesan

Department of Mathematics, Vel Tech Rangarajan Dr. Sagunthala R&D Institute of Science and Technology, Avadi, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Subashini Chandrasekar

Department of Chemistry, Rajalakshmi Institute of Technology, Poonamlee, Chennai, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-13

13.1 Introduction

The accumulation of abnormal or excessive fat causing excessive weight is termed as obesity. It creates a significant risk factor in human health. Obesity is based on a measure known as body mass index, which indicates the amount of body fat. The issue has become so serious that, day by day, the count of people who die due to obesity has increased. In addition, obesity changes the physical appearance of a person, which in turn affects both their mental and physical health. Hence, it is absolutely necessary to study the factors causing obesity so that it can be addressed before it becomes a critical issue.

Various researchers have shown their findings with respect to obesity. Anand Anbarasu in 2011 gave an overview on obesity and its critical effects in humans [1]. Rodolfo Canas et al. in 2020 performed an estimation on various levels of obesity with the help of computational intelligence [2]. Adeyemi Okunogbe et al. in 2021 considered eight different countries for their research work. They assessed the effects of being overweight in the current scenario and also provided future predictions [3]. Mahmood Safaei et al. in 2021 studied obesity to understand the reasons behind it and the consequences faced, using machine learning as a tool [4]. Graciela C. Alatorre-Cruz et al. in 2022 studied the effect of obesity using a mathematical progression method [5].

In recent times. machine learning has attracted many researchers. A few of them are shown here for reference. Shixia Liu et al. in 2017 presented their analysis using machine learning [6]. Yue Liu, in the same year, designed machine learning methods to discover materials for their work [7]. Aishwarya Mujumdar et al. in 2019 conducted a prediction analysis for diabetes using machine learning [8]. KM Jyoti Rani in 2020 also performed a prediction analysis to detect diabetes with the help of machine language [9]. Iqbal H. Sarker in 2021 discussed various machine learning applications in real-time situations [10]. V. Umarani et al. in 2021 conducted an analysis using machine learning techniques based on sentiments [11]. Regan Murugesan et al. in 2023 studied COVID variants with the help of cognitive maps [12]. Shubham Sain et al. in 2023 used machine learning to analyze diabetes [13]. This chapter aims to analyze obesity using machine learning.

13.2 Standard Definitions

Various definitions applied in this chapter are given below:

  • Obesity: A disease caused by an excessive accumulation of body fat.
  • Body Mass Index: A measure applied to adults based on the height and weight of the person to calculate body fat is body mass index.
  • Machine Learning: A field of study in which computers get the ability to learn with programming technique is machine learning.
  • MATLAB: The term refers to matrix laboratory. Arrays and matrices are the basics that operate on MATLAB. It is based on a programming system used to analyze data.
  • True Positive (TP): Refers to the correct positive prediction.
  • False Positive (FP): Refers to the incorrect positive prediction.
  • True Negative (TN): Refers to the correct negative prediction.
  • False Negative (FN): Refers to the incorrect negative prediction.
  • Confusion Matrix: The performance of a classification algorithm is visualized using it. It is represented in tabulated form, summarizing the number of positive and negative predictions. The format of confusion matrix is given in Table 13.1 below.
  • Accuracy: Refers to the ratio that represents the number of all correctly predicted values to the total values. The higher the value of accuracy, the higher likelihood the predicted value is true.

$Accuracy = \frac{TN + TP\ }{TN + TP + FP + FN}$

  • Precision: Refers to the number of actually positive values from all the positively predicted class values. Precision also shows a good result, when its value is higher.

$Precision = \frac{TP}{TP + FP}$

  • Recall: Refers to the number of correctly predicted value, from all the positive classes. Higher recall values indicate a higher likelihood the predicted value is true.

$Recall = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$

  • F1-Score: The harmonic mean of recall together with precision is referred by this score. It take values between 0 and 1.

$F1\mspace{9mu} Score = \frac{2}{\frac{1}{Precision} + \frac{1}{Recall}}$

13.3 Obesity

Obesity, a complex disease is confirmed based on the measure of various definitions applied in this chapter as given below.

  • Obesity: A disease caused due an excessive accumulation of body fat
  • Body Mass Index: A measure applied to adults based on the height and weight of the person to calculate the body fat is body mass index.
  • Machine Learning: A field of study in which the computers get the ability to learn without programming technique is machine learning.
  • MATLAB: It refers to matrix laboratory. Arrays and matrices are the basic that operates on MATLAB. It is based on a programming system used to analyze data.
  • True Positive (TP): It refers to the correct positive prediction.
  • False Positive (FP): It refers to the incorrect positive prediction.
  • True Negative (TN): It refers to the correct negative prediction.
  • False Negative (FN): It refers to the incorrect negative prediction.
  • Confusion Matrix: The performance of a classification algorithm is visualized using it. It is represented in tabulated form, summarizing the number of positive and negative predictions. The format of confusion matrix is given in Table 13.1.
  • Accuracy: It refers to the ratio of representing the number of all correctly predicted values to the total values. Higher the value of accuracy, higher the predicted value is true.

$Accuracy = \frac{TN + TP\ }{TN + TP + FP + FN}$

  • Precision: It refers to the number of actually positive values from all the positively predicted class values, Precision also shows a good result, when its value is higher.

$Precision = \frac{TP}{TP + FP}$

  • Recall: It refers to the number of correctly predicted value, from all the positive classes. Higher the recall values denotes higher the predicted value is true.

$Recall = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$

  • F1-Score: The harmonic mean of recall together with precision is referred by this score. It take values between 0 and 1.

$F1\mspace{9mu} Score = \frac{2}{\frac{1}{Precision} + \frac{1}{Recall}}$

Body Mass Index. The stage prior to obesity is termed as overweight. Based on the body mass index, a person is concluded to be underweight or overweight or obese. Body mass index is measured in units of kg/m2. When the body mass index value becomes less than 18.5, it indicates underweight, the range of 18.5 to 24.9 is termed as normal weight, which is advisable. The value between 25 to 29.9 is termed as overweight. When a person's body mass index value becomes greater than 30, the person is said to have obesity.

Obesity, based on the body mass index of more than 30, is classified into three classes. A value between 30 to 34.9 is said to be of class I, a value between 35 to 39.9 is said to be of class II and a value greater than 40 is said to be of class III.

13.4 Factors Causing Obesity

There are various factors behind obesity. Some of them are listed with higher weightage and are discussed in this section.

In this world of the internet, people run after earning physical assets. In this pursuit, people forget to prioritize homemade food. Most people in corporate culture are interested in junk food. The habit of eating without following any proper timing is becoming common nowadays.

Genes also remains a reason behind obesity. People from the same family are found to have obesity. In some people with health disorder, the medications taken for their illness contribute to weight gain. Due to work, people do not even find time to sleep for 8 hours, which is suggested for good health. The physical activity of individuals has also been reduced because their time is spent on work. Various such factors remain the reason behind obesity.

This chapter aims to make predictions on obesity based on the factors affecting it. The attributes chosen for the predictive analysis are listed as below:

  • Junk food intake
  • Sleep more than 8 hours
  • Do physical exercise
  • Genetics
  • Illness, medication

13.5 Methodology

With the aim of predicting the factors involved in obesity, it is proposed to conduct a survey on the general public to identify various activities related to the attributes mentioned above. A Google form with the list of following questionnaires was circulated to collect data for the analysis. All the questions are given in objective type questions with answers of ‘yes’ or ‘no’. The sixth question serves as the decision attribute, which aids in performing the proposed predictive analysis.

  1. Do you take junk food?
  2. Do you regularly get 8 hours of sleep?
  3. Do you participate in physical exercise?
  4. Does any of your relative have obesity?
  5. Do you take any medication for illness on a regular basis?
  6. Do you have obesity?

The responses related to the survey questionnaire are recorded from respondents across various age groups.

13.6 MATLAB

The proposed methodology is then analyzed using machine learning concept with MATLAB. In order to make the analysis, factors such as confusion matrix, precision. accuracy, F1-score and recall are calculated using MATLAB. The major part of the MATLAB coding is provided below for reference in Table 13.2.

Table 13.2 MATLAB Coding for Obesity Prediction Analysis

confusion_value_matrix = confusionmat(true_response, predicted_response);

true_value_positive = confusion_value_matrix(1, 1);

false_value_positive = confusion_value_matrix(2, 1);

false_value_negative = confusion_value_matrix(1, 2);

true_value_negative = confusion_value_matrix(2, 2);

accuracy = (true_value_positive + true_value_negative) / sum(confusion_value_matrix(:));

precision = true_value_positive / (true_value_positive + false_value_positive);

recall = true_value_positive / (true_value_positive + false_value_negative);

f1_score = 2 * (recall * precision) / (recall + precision);

The responses obtained from decision attribute is compared with the responses received for all other attributes, and the specified factors for the analysis are calculated. The output obtained after the execution of the coding is presented in Table 13.3. The values obtained from the output are listed in Table 13.4 for easy reference.

Depending on the attributes specified, the change in attribute values is found to have their individual values. The graphical representation of the values obtained is presented below in Figure 13.1.

With respect to the values of accuracy, it is found that junk food intake has the highest values, followed by physical exercise. Next are the illness and medication attributes. The genetic and sleep time attributes have almost the same values. When it comes to the precision values, similar to accuracy, precision is higher for junk food intake. Next comes physical exercise followed by genetics, illness and medication with equal weightage. Precision has the lowest value regarding sleeping hours. Regarding recall, genetic factor takes the highest value, followed by illness and medication. Next are sleeping time, physical exercise and junk food intake in order. Considering the values obtained for F1 score, genetics takes the highest value, followed by illness and medication. Then comes physical exercise, followed by junk food intake. Sleeping hours takes the lowest value in terms of the F1-score aspect.

The values indicate that junk food and sleeping time have a major impact on creating obesity with respect to accuracy and precision. Illness and medication, together with genetic factors, show a major impact on recall and F1-score. Physical exercise shows less impact on all four categories. Precautions should be taken in these aspects to prevent obesity.

13.7 Conclusion

Predictive analysis of various attributes related to obesity is carried out using MATLAB software. The values involved in the analysis, such as F1-score, accuracy, recall and precision are calculated using the confusion matrix obtained. The attribute with the highest weightage is identified. The attributes for which precaution need to be taken to lead a healthy life without being affected by obesity are identified.
