14 Intuitionistic Fuzzy Dombi-Archimedean Weighted Aggregation Operators and Their Applications in Sustainable Material Selection

Abhijit Saha

Assistant Professor (Research), Department of Computing Technologies, SRM Institute of Science and Technology (SRMIST), Kattankulathur, Tamil Nadu, India

P. Sumitra

Associate Professor, PG and Research Department of Computer Science and Applications, Vivekanandha College of Arts and Science for Women (Autonomous), Tiruchengode, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-14

14.1 Introduction

The idea of FSs (Zadeh, 1965) was developed primarily as a result of taking confusing human judgments into account while resolving practical issues. The FSs philosophy, on the other hand, may control the reality emerging from computational observation and comprehension, which includes things like ambiguity, partial belongingness, inaccuracy, etc. Later, Atanassov (1986) presented the Intuitionistic fuzzy set (IFS) as a generalization of FS to deal with situations with incomplete data by using an non-belongingness grade. We are aware that the concept of IFSs is among the pioneering commitments has so far been recorded. Mukherjee (2017) selected the best fuel technology for land transportation subject to multiple criteria resulting in a sustainable transportation system. Büyüközkan et al. (2018) evaluated different public bus technologies as urban transportation alternatives. Wang et al. (2018) assessed consumer satisfaction in urban rail travel. Mishra et al. (2019) solved a multi-criteria IT personnel selection problem with IF measures and ARAS tool. Mishra et al. (2020) utilized IF-EDAS approach for assessment of health-care waste disposal technologies. Kumari and Mishra (2020) used IF-parametric measures and IF-COPRAS for selecting green suppliers. Rouyendegh et al. (2020) utilized IF-TOPSIS method for green supplier selection. Rani et al. (2021) assessed the performance of telecom service providers using IF-grey relational analysis.

Buran and Erçek (2022) presented a business model canvas framework for public transportation organizations. Yan et al. (2022) provided a framework to determine the priority of a rail transit system from the perspective of green and low carbon. Palanisami et al. (2022) developed a new approach of multi-modal medical image fusion using IF sets. Deb et al. (2022) developed a decision-making model for the selection of enterprise resource planning systems. Gohain et al. (2022) developed a symmetric distance formula and applied it to practical problems of decision-making, pattern recognition, and clustering problems. Rasoulzadeh et al. (2022) introduced a multi-objective approach to the portfolio selection problem. Senapati et al. (2022) developed an advanced decision-support model for the prioritization of sustainable transportation-sharing practices.

All of the given information can be combined into a single one using aggregate operators (AOs). The generalizations of a large number of other t-Nms and t-Cnms are known as archimedean t-norms (t-Nms) and t-conorms (t-Cnms). However, the Dombi AOs, which are just t-Nm and t-Cnm operations, have the advantage of having a high degree of parameter freedom (s). Works on IF-AOs is catching momemtum recently. For aggregating IF data, many AOs (Xu, 2007; Zhao & Wei, 2013; Garg, 2019; Seikh & Mandal, 2021; Senapati et al., 2022) have been used up until this point. But all of them are lacking in both generality and flexibility together. So, the development of a hybrid AO is an urgent need for desired generality and flexibility during aggregation process. So far no work has been done by merging IFSs, Dombi and Archimedean operators (Liu et al., 2020; Saha et al., 2021; Baidya et al., 2021; Liu et al., 2022). This work is related to the development of IFDAWA and IFDAWG operators which take into account both generality and flexibility.

Section 14.2 provides an explanation of the essential concepts underlying IFSs, Archimedean operations, and Dombi operations. Section 14.3 deals with Dombi-Archimedean operations. Section 14.4 discusses the growth of the IFDAWA and IFDAWG operators. The following section deals with collective decision-making utilizing IF data. Section 14.5 evaluates and chooses a suitable sustainable material using the provided methods. A comparison with current operators is made in Section 14.6 to demonstrate the viability of the suggested methodology. The study's summary and conclusion are presented in Section 14.7 and Section 14.8.

14.2 Basic Concepts

Definition 14.2.1 (Atanassov, 1986): “An IFS ζ on Γ is described by ζ = {⟨yi, μ(yi), ν(yi)⟩|yi ∈ Γ}, μ, ν:Γ→[0, 1] being the membership and non-membership functions, respectively, satisfying 0 ≤ μ(yi) + ν(yi) ≤ 1. Also we use πζ(yi) = 1 − μζ(yi) − νζ(yi). ζ reduces to a IF number (IFN) if Γ is singleton and we write ζ =  < μ, ν>, for μ, ν ∈ [0, 1] and 0 ≤ μ + ν ≤ 1.

Definition 14.2.2 (Xu et al., 2015; Deb et al., 2022): Consider an IFN ζ =  < μ, ν > >, Then:

$\begin{array}{l} {\mathbb{S}_{\zeta} = \frac{1}{2}(1 + \mu - \nu),} \\ {\mathbb{A}_{\zeta} = \mu + \nu,} \\ \end{array}$ (14.1)

define “score value” and “accuracy value” respectively of ζ, where 𝔸ζ ∈ [0, 1] and 𝕊ζ ∈ [0, 1]. For the IFNs ζ1 =  < μ1, ν1> and ζ2 =  < μ2, ν2>, a ranking rule is:

  1. $\zeta_{1} \succ \zeta_{2}\left| \begin{array}{l} {\mathbb{S}_{\zeta_{1}} > \mathbb{S}_{\zeta_{2}},} \\ {\mathbb{S}_{\zeta_{1}} = \mathbb{S}_{\zeta_{2}},\mspace{9mu}\mathbb{A}_{\zeta_{1}} > \mathbb{A}_{\zeta_{2}}} \\ \end{array} \right.$
  2. ζ1 = ζ2 | 𝕊ζ1 = 𝕊ζ2, 𝔸ζ1 = 𝔸ζ2.

Definition 14.2.3 (Deb et al., 2022): For the IFNs ζ1 =  < μ1, ν1> and ζ2 =  < μ2, ν2>, the basic operations are:

  1. ζ1c =  < ν1, μ1>,
  2. ζ1 ⊕ ζ2 = ⟨μ1 + μ2 − μ1μ2, ν1ν2⟩,
  3. ζ1 ⊗ ζ2 = ⟨μ1μ2, ν1 + ν2 − ν1ν2⟩,
  4. λζ1 = ⟨1 − (1 − μ1)λ, ν1λ⟩ (λ > 0),
  5. ζ1λ = ⟨μ1λ, 1 − (1 − ν1)λ⟩ (λ > 0).

Definition 14.2.4 (Klement & Mesiar, 2005): A strictly Archimedean t-norm is nothing but a strictly deceasing continuous map σ:(0,1]→R defined as:

σ − 1(σ(x) + σ(y)) for x, y ∈ (0, 1] (provided σ(1) = 0)

Definition 14.2.5 (Klement & Mesiar, 2005): A strictly Archimedean t-conorm is a map τ:[0,1)→R defined by:

τ − 1(τ(x) + τ(y)) for x, y ∈ [0, 1) (provided τ(l) = σ(1 − l), l ∈ [0, 1))

Definition 14.2.6 (Dombi, 1982): The expressions for the Dombi t-norm and Dombi t-conorm are:

$Dom(a,b) = \frac{1}{1 + \left\{ {\left( \frac{1 - a}{a} \right)^{P} + \left( \frac{1 - b}{b} \right)^{P}} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}},Dom^{c}(a,b) = 1 - \frac{1}{1 + \left\{ {\left( \frac{a}{1 - a} \right)^{P} + \left( \frac{b}{1 - b} \right)^{P}} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}}\mspace{9mu}(P \geq 1)$

(where a, b ∈ [0, 1])”

14.3 Dombi-Archimedean Operations Between IFNs

Definition 14.3.1 “Suppose ζ1 =  < μ1, ν1> and ζ2 =  < μ2, ν2> are two IFNs and $\vartheta_{P}(t) = {(\frac{t}{1 - t})}^{P},\ \varphi_{P}(s) = {(\frac{1 - s}{s})}^{P},\mspace{9mu} t \in \lbrack 0,1),s \in (0,1\rbrack\mspace{9mu}{and}\mspace{9mu} P \geq 1$. Then the Dombi-Archimedean (DA) operations are:

  1. $\zeta_{1}\widetilde{\oplus}\zeta_{2} = \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {1 - \frac{1}{1 + {\{{\tau{({\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\mu_{1})) + \tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\mu_{2}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}},} \\ \frac{1}{1 + {\{{\sigma{({\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\nu_{1})) + \sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\nu_{2}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}} \\ \end{array} \right\rangle$
  2. $\zeta_{1}\widetilde{\otimes}\zeta_{2} = \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {\frac{1}{1 + {\{{\sigma{({\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\mu_{1})) + \sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\mu_{2}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}},} \\ {1 - \frac{1}{1 + {\{{\tau{({\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\nu_{1})) + \tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\nu_{2}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}}} \\ \end{array} \right\rangle$
  3. $\lambda\zeta_{1} = \left\langle {1 - \frac{1}{1 + {\{{\tau{({\lambda\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\mu_{1}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}},\frac{1}{1 + {\{{\sigma{({\lambda\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\nu_{1}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}}} \right\rangle\quad(\lambda > 0)$
  4. $\zeta_{1}^{\lambda} = \left\langle {\frac{1}{1 + {\{{\sigma{({\lambda\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\mu_{1}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}},1 - \frac{1}{1 + {\{{\tau{({\lambda\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\nu_{1}))})}}\}}^{\frac{1}{P}}}} \right\rangle\quad(\lambda > 0)$

Theorem 14.3.1

For the IFNs αj = (μj, νj) (j = 1, 2), we have

  1. α1 ⊕ α2 = α2 ⊕ α1
  2. α1 ⊕ α2 = α2 ⊕ α1
  3. λ(α1 ⊕ α2) = (λα1) ⊕ (λα2)
  4. (α1 ⊕ α2)λ = (α1λ) ⊕ (α2λ)
  5. (λ1 + λ2)α1 = (λ1α1) ⊕ (λ2α1)
  6. α1(λ1 + λ2) = (α1λ1) ⊕ (α1λ2)

Proof: Follows from Definition 14.3.1.”

14.4 IF Dombi-Archimedean Weighted AOs

Definition 14.4.1 “Suppose ζm =  < μm, νm> (m = 1, 2, ..., t) be a set of IFNs and Wm denotes the weight of ζm satisfying 0 ≤ Wm ≤ 1 and $\sum\limits_{m = 1}^{t}W_{m} = 1.$ Then the IFDAWA operator is presented as:

$IFDAWA(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{t}) = \underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\widetilde{\oplus}}}{({W_{m}\zeta_{m}})}.$ (14.2)

Theorem 14.4.1 IFDAWA(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) is also an IFN. In addition, we get:

$\begin{array}{l} {IFDAWA(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{t})} \\ {= \left\langle {1 - \left( {1 + \left\{ {\tau\left( {\underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\sum}}W_{m}\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\mu_{m}))} \right)} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}} \right)^{- 1},\mspace{9mu}\left( {1 + \left\{ {\sigma\left( {\underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\sum}}W_{m}\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\nu_{m}))} \right)} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}} \right)^{- 1}} \right\rangle} \\ \end{array}$ (14.3)

Proof: Follows from Definition 14.4.1.

FFDAWA operator has the following properties.

Theorem 14.4.2 (Shift invariance): If ζ0( ≠ ζm(m = 1, 2, ..., t)) is an IFN, then IFDAWA(ζ0 ⊕ ζ1, ζ0 ⊕ ζ2, ..., ζ0 ⊕ ζt) = ζ0 ⊕ IFDAWA(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt).

Theorem 14.4.3 (Idempotency): If ζ0( ≠ ζm(m = 1, 2, ..., t)) is an IFN, then IFDAWA(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) = ζ0.

Theorem 14.4.4 (Boundedness): ζ− ≺ IFDAWA(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) ≺ ζ+, where

$\zeta^{-} = < \min\limits_{m}\mu_{m},\mspace{9mu}\max\limits_{m}\nu_{m} > \mspace{9mu}{and}\mspace{9mu}\zeta^{+} = < \max\limits_{m}\mu_{m},\mspace{9mu}\min\limits_{m}\nu_{m} > \mspace{-5mu}.$

Theorem 14.4.5 (Monotonicity): For another collection of IFNs ζ′m =  < μ′m, ν′m> (m = 1, 2, ..., t), satisfying μm ≤ μ′m, νm ≥ ν′m (m = 1, 2, ..., t), ∀ j, we have IFDAWA(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) ≺ IFDAWA(ζ′1, ζ′2, ..., ζ′t).

Definition 14.4.2 Suppose ζm =  < μm, νm> (m = 1, 2, ..., t) be a set of IFNs and Wm denotes the weight of ζm satisfying 0 ≤ Wm ≤ 1 and $\sum\limits_{m = 1}^{t}{W_{m} = 1.}$ Then the IFDAWG operator is presented as:

$IFDAWG(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{t}) = \underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\overset{\frown}{\otimes}}}{({\zeta_{m}}^{W_{m}})}.$ (14.4)

Theorem 14.4.6 IFDAWG(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) is also an IFN. In addition, we get:

$\begin{array}{l} {IFDAWG(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{t})} \\ {= \left\langle {\left( {1 + \left\{ {\sigma\left( {\underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\sum}}W_{m}\sigma^{- 1}(\varphi_{P}(\mu_{m}))} \right)} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}} \right)^{- 1},1 - \left( {1 + \left\{ {\tau\left( {\underset{m = 1}{\overset{t}{\sum}}W_{m}\tau^{- 1}(\vartheta_{P}(\nu_{m}))} \right)} \right\}^{\frac{1}{P}}} \right)^{- 1}} \right\rangle} \\ \end{array}$ (14.5)

Proof: Follows from Definition 14.4.2.

IFDAWG operator obeys the following laws.

Theorem 14.4.7 (Shift invariance): If $\zeta_{0}( \neq \zeta_{m}(m = 1,2,\mspace{-2mu}...,\mspace{9mu} t))$ is an IFN, then

IFDAWG(ζ0 ⊕ ζ1, ζ0 ⊕ ζ2, ..., ζ0 ⊕ ζt) = ζ0 ⊕ IFDAWG(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt).

Theorem 14.4.8 (Idempotency): If ζ0( ≠ ζm (m = 1, 2, ..., t)) is an IFN, then

IFDAWG(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) = ζ0.

Theorem 14.4.9 (Boundedness): ζ− ≺ IFDAWG(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) ≺ ζ+, where

$\zeta^{-} = < \min\limits_{m}\mu_{m},\mspace{9mu}\max\limits_{m}\nu_{m} > \mspace{9mu}{and}\mspace{9mu}\zeta^{+} = < \max\limits_{m}\mu_{m},\mspace{9mu}\min\limits_{m}\nu_{m} > .$

Theorem 14.4.10 (Monotonicity): If ζm =  < μm, νm> and ζ′m =  < μ′m, ν′m> (m = 1, 2, ..., t), are two collections of IFNs satisfying μm ≤ μ′m, νm ≥ ν′m (m = 1, 2, ..., t), ∀ j, then IFDAWG(ζ1, ζ2, ..., ζt) ≺ IFDAWG(ζ′1, ζ′2, ..., ζ′t).

14.5 Decision-Making Methodology

  1. Step 1. Construct the initial IF decision matrices.

“Assume that m is the number of alternatives Ai (i = 1, 2, ..., m) and n is the number of criteria Cj (j = 1, 2, ..., n) connected with a group decision-making issue with experts Er (r = 1, 2, ..., l). Experts initial findings are basically IF matrices of the form Mr = [ζr(ij)]m × n = [ < μr(ij), νr(ij)>]m × n.

  1. Step 2. Use IFDAWA (or IFDAWG) operator for aggregation.

The aggregated matrix is [ζ(ij)]m × n = [ < μ(ij), ν(ij)>]m × n, where:

$\begin{array}{l} {\zeta^{(ij)} = IFDAWA(\zeta_{1}^{(ij)},\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{2}^{(ij)},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{l}^{(ij)})\quad(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m;\mspace{9mu} j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n),} \\ {\ {or},} \\ {\zeta^{(ij)} = IFDAWG(\zeta_{1}^{(ij)},\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{2}^{(ij)},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta_{l}^{(ij)})\quad(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m;\mspace{9mu} j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n),} \\ \end{array}$ (14.6)

where Wr is the weight of the decision-maker Er (r = 1, 2, ..., l).

  1. Step 3. Normalize [ζ(ij)]m × n.

Suppose that the normalized [ζ(ij)]m × n is [ζ̃(ij)]m × n = [ < μ̃(ij), ν̃(ij)>]m × n, where:”

${\widetilde{\zeta}}^{(ij)} = \left\{ {\begin{array}{l} {< \mu^{(ij)},\mspace{9mu}\nu^{(ij)} > \mspace{-5mu},\mspace{9mu}{if}\ C_{j}\ {is}\mspace{9mu}{beneficial}\mspace{9mu}{criteria}} \\ {< \nu^{(ij)},\mspace{9mu}\mu^{(ij)} > \mspace{-5mu},\mspace{9mu}{if}\ C_{j}\mspace{9mu}{is}\mspace{9mu}{non} - {beneficial}\mspace{9mu}{criteria}} \\ \end{array}\mspace{9mu}(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m;\mspace{9mu} j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n).} \right.$ (14.7)

  1. Step 4 Estimation of the criteria weights.

The notion of entropy has been widely employed in the theory of uncertainty as it measures the degree of informational uncertainty. Motivated by this, we use the entropy measure to determine weights of the criteria.

  1. Step 4.1. Obtain the normalized score matrix.

The normalized score matrix [𝕊(ζ̃(ij))]m × n is derived using the following equation:

$\mathbb{S}\left( {\widetilde{\zeta}}^{(ij)} \right) = \frac{1 + {\widetilde{\mu}}^{(ij)} - {\widetilde{\nu}}^{(ij)}}{2}\mspace{9mu}(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m;\mspace{9mu} j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n).$ (14.8)

  1. Step 4.2. The entropy value Δj corresponding to the criterion Cj is clarified by:

$\Delta_{j} = - \frac{1}{\ln(m)}\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{m}{\mathbb{S}({\widetilde{\zeta}}^{(ij)}) \times \ \ln(\mathbb{S}({\widetilde{\zeta}}^{(ij)}))(j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n).}$ (14.9)

  1. Step 4.3. The objective weight of the criteria is ascertained as:

$w_{j} = \frac{|{1 - \Delta_{j}}|}{\sum\limits_{k = 1}^{n}{|{1 - \Delta_{k}}|}}\mspace{9mu}(j = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} n).$ (14.10)

where ψ = (ψ1, …, ψj, …, ψn)T is the importance vector of the criteria with ψj ∈ [0, 1] (j = 1, 2, ..., n), and $\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{n}{\psi_{j} = 1.}$

  1. Step 5. Employ IFDAWA (or IFADWG) operator to get the final output.

The final aggregated matrix is [ζ(i)]m × 1 = [ < μ(i), ν(i)>]m × 1, where:

$\begin{array}{l} {\zeta^{(i)} = IFDAWA(\zeta^{(i1)},\zeta^{(i2)},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta^{(in)})\mspace{9mu}(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m),} \\ {\ {or},} \\ {\zeta^{(i)} = IFDAWG(\zeta^{(i1)}\ ,\zeta^{(i2)},...,\mspace{9mu}\zeta^{(in)})\mspace{9mu}(i = 1,2,...,\mspace{9mu} m),} \\ \end{array}$ (14.11)

  1. Step 6. Generate the ranking of choices Ai (i = 1, 2, ..., m) and pick the best one.

14.6 Case Study and Its Solution

The choice of sustainable materials (SMS) is a vital step in the design-manufacturing process. When choosing materials incorrectly, a company's production, profitability, and reputation may all suffer. Currently, some of the materials most frequently utilized for trash cans include aluminium (Al), ABS plastic (ABS), and polyurethane (PU). When selecting a material for production, it is important to choose the proper product recovery and disposal techniques, such as recycling and landfilling (LND), recycling and incineration (INC), and recycling and remanufacturing (REM). The material options are Al-REM (A1), PU-LND (A2), ABS-INC (A3), and PU-INC (A4). For evaluation, we take 14 criteria. They are: elongation at break (B1), tensile strength (B2), density (B3), raw material extraction (B4), potential for recycling and reuse (B5), energy saving (B6), tax contribution (B7), rigidity (B8), CO2 emission (B9), usage of water (B10), tensile modulus (B11), disposal cost (B12,), maintenance cost (B13), and initial cost (B14). Out of these, B8 to B14 are non-beneficial criteria. Table 14.1 displays the initial evaluations made by the experts.

To obtain a desired outcome, we implement the proposed IF decision support model. Assume that experts’ weights are respectively 0.2693, 0.2965, 0.1982, and 0.2360. Then, the aggregated (taking P=2) FF decision matrix (Table 14.2) is obtained by using the IFDAWA operator. Next, we prepare the normalized aggregated matrix and its corresponding score matrix. Then, the entropy values are determined by using Eq. (14.9). Next, attribute weights are computed (see Tables 14.3 and 14.4 for outcomes). Then, we obtain the final aggregated IFNs using the IFDAWA operator (taking P=2). These are: ζ(1) =  < 0.4676, 0.5151>, ζ(2) =  < 0.4618, 0.5099>, ζ(3) =  < 0.4685, 0.5107>, ζ(4) =  < 0.4762, 0.4617>. Lastly, scores of these aggregated IFNs are computed according to which A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A1 ≻ A2 (“≻” means “superior to”) is preference order with A4 as the most suitable option.

14.7 Comparative Analysis

IF weighted algebraic and geometric AOs based method (Xu, 2007), IF Dombi weighted algebraic and geometric AOs based method (Seikh & Mandal, 2021), IF Hamacher weighted algebraic and geometric AOs based method (Garg, 2019), IF Aczel-Alsina weighted algebraic and geometric AOs based method (Senapati et al., 2022) and IF Einstein weighted algebraic and geometric AOs based method (Zhao & Wei, 2013) have been considered for applying the same case study considered in section 2.5. Outcomes are respectively, A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A2 ≻ A1, A4 ≻ A2 ≻ A3 ≻ A1, A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A2 ≻ A1, A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A2 ≻ A1 and A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A2 ≻ A1. But the proposed one gives A4 ≻ A3 ≻ A1 ≻ A2 because of the presence of both Dombi and Archimedean operations. The advantages are: (a) the current AOs (Xu, 2007; Zhao & Wei, 2013; Garg, 2019; Seikh & Mandal, 2021; Senapati et al., 2022) are less useful for aggregating IF data as they are lacking in flexibility and generality. But the developed AOs are free from this critical issue.

14.8 Conclusion

In comparison to fuzzy and hesitant fuzzy sets, IFS offers a sizable benefit, making it a practical tool for handling uncertain information. In this paper, some IF Dombi-Archimedean (DA) AOs are suggested to overcome decision-making issues. The only IF AOs currently accessible for data aggregation are the algebraic, Dombi, Einstein, Hamacher, and Yager operators. These IF AOs are not flexible or sufficiently general. We were motivated to develop the DA AOs in the IF environment by these issues. We describe the core characteristics of the proposed AOs, including idempotency, boundedness, and monotonicity. Then, a decision-making model is supplied with IF data. The criteria weights in this example are estimated using an entropy-based algorithm. Comparison results show that the proposed model is very effective under IF setting. Pythagorean and Fermatean fuzzy sets can be used in the future to address other decision-making issues, such as warehouse site selection (Saha et al., 2023), sustainable supplier selection (Mishra et al., 2022a), renewable energy source selection (Mishra et al., 2022b), assessment of IoT barriers (Mishra et al., 2022c), and sustainable third-party reverse logistics provider selection (Krishankumar et al., 2021). Moreover, a consensus based methodology can be developed by extending the proposed methodology in order to reduce the impact of biased decision-expert(s).”

15 Identification of Rice Leaf Disease Using Gaussian Mixture Model A Machine Learning Approach Using Image Classification Techniques

Binoy Sasmal

Research Scholar, Department of Computer Science & Engineering, National Institute of Technology, Itanagar, Arunachal Pradesh, India

Rajat Subhra Goswami

Associate Professor, Department of Computer Science & Engineering, National Institute of Technology, Itanagar, Arunachal Pradesh, India

Manas Pratim Dutta

Associate Professor, Department of Computer Science & Information Technology, Cotton University, Guwahati, Assam, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-15

15.1 Introduction

A disease in a plant can be identified as when it is not healthy or when it is unable to operate normally. Diseases in plants are a widespread problem which impacts the overall growth of the plant and its general health. Numerous things, such as bacterial, viral, and fungal pathogens, environmental stressors, and nutrient deficiencies, can contribute to the development of these diseases [1,2].

Some signs of leaf diseases are abnormality in the leaf structure, withering, browning, spots of black or brown color on the leaf, etc. If these diseases are left untreated, they may lead to various problems such as low yield, low growth rate and even death in some cases. Identification of the underlying cause and use of appropriate control methods, such as cultural practices, chemical treatments, and biological controls, are crucial for the prevention and management of leaf diseases [3,4].

15.1.1 Types of Leaf Diseases

Below are some of the most common types of diseases which are found in plants [5,6,7]:

15.1.2 Brown Spot

One of the most common diseases in plants is the brown spot disease; it is caused mainly by the fungal pathogen, Septoria spp. This fungal pathogen produces a brown, circular or angular spot on the leaves of the infected plants. These patches could have a golden halo around the perimeter and a dark brown center.

The most favored condition for brown spot disease is warm, humid weather conditions and it can spread rapidly under these conditions. Figure 15.1 shows rice leaf infected with brown spot. The fungus overwinters on plant debris from sick plants, and during wet weather, spores are formed which can spread the disease to other plants. Brown spot disease can result in defoliation, slowed plant growth, and decreased production if left untreated.

15.1.3 Leaf Smut

Leaf smut disease is a type of fungal disease which affects various types of crops like corn, wheat, barley, etc. This disease is caused by the fungi Entyloma spp., which causes dark, irregular-shaped smut galls or pustules on the leaves of infected plants.

The yellowing or browning of leaves, stunted growth, and decreased yield are all signs of leaf smut disease. In extreme situations, the illness may result in the plant dying before its time.

Leaf smut disease is caused by spores of fungi which spread through the help of wind or rain splash. Here Figure 15.2 represents leaf smut disease in a leaf. The fungus can endure these conditions for several years by overwintering in soil or plant debris that has been infected.

15.1.4 Bacterial Leaf Blight

The bacterial pathogen Xanthomonas campestris is responsible for the widespread plant disease known as bacterial leaf blight. It affects a wide range of plants, including vegetables, citrus, rice, and soybeans.

Bacterial leaf blight can be characterized by water-soaked lesions on the leaves, which can swiftly turn brown or black and occasionally have a yellow halo around the edge. In Figure 15.3, it has been shown that the leaf is infected with bacterial leaf blight. In extreme circumstances, the leaves may turn necrotic and pass away.

This disease is mostly spread by wind, rain splash, or through contact with dead plant debris which were previously infected with a similar disease. Controlling the illness is challenging because it can persist for years in plant debris or soil [6].

15.1.5 Rust

A fungus that affects plants and results in patches on their leaves that are yellow, orange, or reddish-brown. It is frequently brought on by high humidity and moisture levels and can damage a wide variety of plant species.

15.1.6 Powdery Mildew

A fungus that affects plants and results in a white or grey powdery coating on their leaves, stems, and flowers. Wide-ranging plant species are susceptible to it, which is frequently brought on by high humidity and inadequate air circulation.

15.1.7 Anthracnose

A fungus that results in dark or black blemishes on plant leaves. It can damage many different plant types and is frequently brought on by rainy weather.

15.1.8 Regions in India Where Diseases Are Mostly Found

In India, leaf diseases can affect plants because they are influenced by a variety of elements, including weather, plant species, soil characteristics, and management techniques. However, leaf diseases are more common in some parts of India than others.

These are the humid and wet regions of coastal areas such as the western and eastern coasts that is, Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Odisha, etc. These regions are more prone to leaf diseases caused by bacterial and fungal pathogens. In contrast, dry regions with low humidity like Rajasthan, Gujrat, and parts of Maharashtra suffer from a variety of leaf diseases which are caused by environmental factors like scarcity of water and high temperature, soil, etc. [7].

15.1.9 How Do These Diseases Affect the Plant

Leaf diseases can affect the plant in a variety of ways depending on what type of disease it is and how much severity of the infection. Some of the ways the disease can affect the plant are:

15.1.10 Wilting and Yellowing

There are several leaf illnesses that can make leaves wilt or turn yellow. This frequently happens because of harm to the plant's vascular tissue, which can obstruct the flow of nutrients and water. This also affects the photosynthesis of the plant which is essential for production of energy in a plant.

15.1.11 Stunted Growth

In extreme circumstances, leaf diseases might result in the plant's mortality or restricted growth. This might happen if the illness spreads to other sections of the plant or if it affects a sizable portion of the leaves.

15.1.12 Reduced Yield

Some leaf diseases can also reduce the overall yield of the plant, particularly if the disease occurs in the early stages of growth of the plant.

15.1.13 Lesions

Some leaf diseases can cause lesions which may be seen as small spots or larger patches on the surface of the leaves. These lesions can cause tissue necrosis or death of plant cells, which can weaken the plant's overall ability to produce and grow.

15.2 Related Work

The concept of smart farming is gaining popularity in the agricultural industry, as it uses various sensors and machine learning technologies to improve the efficiency of the farm. According to a survey, over 50% of the industry is experiencing significant losses due to the emergence of diseases on plant leaves. It's important to monitor and prevent the spread of these diseases in order to boost agricultural yields. We can use algorithms to detect diseases on leaves. In this study, we compare the three different models that are used in this field. The CNN model is able to perform better than the SVM and KNN models when it comes to detecting various leaf diseases. When trained with images of soybean leaf diseases, the CNN model is able to achieve an accuracy rate of 96%. It performs better than the SVM and KNN models, which have an accuracy rate of 76% and 64%, respectively [1].

The growth of India's population and increasing food demands have resulted in the need for more agricultural activities. This sector plays a vital role in the country's economy. Unfortunately, the lack of yield can be caused by various diseases, such as fungi, bacteria, and viruses. Detection techniques can help prevent these diseases from affecting the crop. In order to identify and detect plant diseases, machine learning techniques are commonly used. These techniques mainly rely on the collected information and offer various effective and efficient methods. For instance, they can analyses data for a specific task and provide a superior result. This paper aims to review the various techniques that are used in the detection of plant diseases using deep learning and machine learning. In addition, deep learning has gained immense popularity in the field of computer vision. This technology has the potential to provide a superior performance in detecting plant diseases. The various applications of deep learning have been widely studied and adopted in the computer vision and machine learning domains. The paper presents a comparative analysis of the two techniques, focusing on their performance in different research papers. Deep learning models can be utilized to identify and detect leaf diseases, which can help prevent major crop losses [2].

Economic losses can be caused by post-harvest diseases of apple. To help prevent these diseases from affecting the apple industry, we developed a web-based diagnostic system called DSSApple. This tool allows users to diagnose different types of apple diseases based on their observed symptoms. Through an image-based stream, the user can interact with the system to visualize the various symptoms of diseases that affect different apple varieties. The user can easily interact with the system by choosing the pictures that represent the various symptoms of the diseases. Through the system's expert-based stream, users can collect feedback about the various characteristics of the diseases that affect a target apple. This method is powered by the use of the Bayesian Networks framework. In order to provide a more accurate and human-friendly diagnosis, we developed a knowledge base that is built on the basis of the observations collected by the system. Through the use of the BN framework, we were able to process the conflicting user feedback and provide explanations for the suggested diagnoses. In two studies, the hybrid approach was thoroughly evaluated. The suggested hybrid version of DSSApple performed better than the single streams and user intuition in terms of accuracy [3,4].

The sugarcane aphid, which is known to cause severe yield loss in the US, is a major threat to the production of sorghum. Although the management of this pest is dependent on the economic threshold levels and monitoring, scouting for it under field conditions is often inefficient and time-consuming. To help improve the efficiency of the program, we developed a deep learning model that can automatically identify the sugarcane aphid on leaves based on their density. Through a combination of field scouting and deep learning techniques, we were able to analyze the performance of four classification models. The models included Inception, Resnet 50, Xception, and the DenseNet 121. Based on the standard criteria for spraying, the models were able to classify the sugarcane aphid into six classes. No sugarcane aphids were found on any plant, and no threat was detected below an action or treatment threshold. They were also able to identify the sugarcane aphids that were above an economic threshold, and they were able to manage them in field conditions. Xception and Inception v3 performed well with an overall accuracy of 86% and a reduced number of misclassified entries. The models were also able to correctly classify the sugarcane aphids at least 97% of the time, demonstrating their potential to be used in remote sensing and sampling protocols. These technologies can help researchers and growers identify and monitor the sugarcane aphids in various resistant and susceptible varieties of sorghum. They can also provide recommendations on the appropriate application of pesticides [7,8].

The economic losses caused by post-harvest diseases of apples can be severe. To help prevent these diseases from affecting the apple industry, we developed DSSApple, an online decision support system that allows users to diagnose and monitor the status of these diseases based on their observed symptoms. This tool is a hybrid diagnostic that can be used by both non-expert and expert users. The system's image-based stream allows users to interact with the system by selecting pictures of various types of diseases. It then compiles user feedback about the disease's characteristics. This method is carried out through a series of questions that are related to the observed symptoms of the disease. The system's expert-based reasoning framework is based on the principles of Bayesian Networks. In this paper, we talk about the steps involved in building a knowledge base for the system. Through the use of the Bayesian Network, we can also process conflicting or incomplete feedback and provide human-readable explanations for the suggested diagnoses. Two studies were conducted on the proposed hybrid approach of DSSApple, which involved simulated and real infected apples. The results of these studies revealed that the proposed system performed better than the single streams and user intuition when it came to diagnosing diseases [9].

The diagnosis and treatment of plant diseases are very important for farmers as they affect the quality and yield of their crops. Unfortunately, visual inspection is often not feasible for most farmers due to the complexity of the situation. An intelligent machine-vision system can help them perform effective and efficient crop inspections. A simple method for identifying and treating plant diseases is to rely on minimal information collected from the images. In a recent study, a new image processing algorithm was used to diagnose and classify different diseases of grapes. The classification process was performed by separating the symptoms from the healthy parts of a leaf using K-means clustering. The features were then extracted from three different color models. The results of the study were then analyzed using the SVM algorithm. The relief features were then selected based on their importance. The suggested method was compared against two deep learning methods: GoogleNet and CNN. The former was able to achieve a classification accuracy of 94.05% and 86.82%, while the latter was able to achieve a classification accuracy of 97.97%. Compared to these two methods, the proposed method had a shorter processing time [10,11].

15.3 Use of Machine Learning for Early Detection

To prevent these harmful diseases Machine Learning techniques are used for early detection. The use of machine learning helps the farmers to detect an abnormality early and more accurately, thus helping them to reduce the overall yield and spread of these diseases to other plantations [8,9].

One of the most common approaches used nowadays is to create a model using machine learning; in this method images from healthy plants and diseased plants are accrued as data. Then the model can be used to classify new images of plant leaves whether they are diseased or healthy based on the visual characteristics of the processed leaves. The data can be accrued through various methods like remote sensing, field survey, or through a smartphone-based application. Figure 15.4 shows the type of machine learning approaches.

Deep learning methods such as Convolution Neural Network (CNN) are also effective for detection of diseases in plant leaves. This method can learn more complex features from the provided images thus it can identify more subtle differences between the diseases and healthy leaves [12].

15.3.1 Methods Used Previously in India

In India, traditional methods for the detection of leaf diseases in plants have included visual inspection by farmers or trained agronomists. The expert would visit the field and then check for visible symptoms of diseases like discoloration, deformity, or lesions. Though this method is effective, it's a time-consuming method and not all abnormalities could be see with the naked eye, so it is susceptible to errors.

Other traditional methods used are the use of chemical sprays or pesticides. To stop or slow the spread of illness, this strategy treats plants chemically. Though, if not used appropriately this technology can be expensive and could have a harmful effect on the environment.

In recent years, a new molecular diagnostic method known as Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) is used for disease detection in leaves. This method involves the amplification and detection of specific DNA sequences from tissue samples, thus, detecting the microorganism responsible for causing the disease at the molecular level. However, this approach can be expensive and necessitates specialized training and laboratory equipment [10].

Though the traditional methods are all effective for detection of leaf disease they can be time consuming, costly and might not be so accurate. Thus, with the introduction of machine learning methods it is more cost effective with more accuracy and speed.

15.3.2 Vertical Farming Role in Machine Learning

The method where the crops are grown vertically in layers while utilizing A.I controlled lightning and climate control systems to achieve the best possible growing conditions is known as vertical farming. This method of farming has several potential advantages, such as higher crop yields, less water use, and enhanced food security.

Optimizing the growing conditions in vertical farming systems can benefit from machine learning. Machine learning algorithms may gather data on variables like temperature, humidity, and light levels using sensors and other monitoring tools, and then utilize this data to modify the growing environment in real time [13].

15.4 Gaussian Matrix Module (GMM)

A Gaussian Matrix Model is a type of mathematical model which involves with the study of random matrices where the entries are distributed according to the Gaussian probability distribution. So, it can be said that the GMM is a random matrix model where the entries of the matrix are independent and identically distributed Gaussian random variables.

GMM is often used as a tool for understanding complex structure, to make the correct prediction and to run simulations where the initial process is too complex to be modeled analytically [14,15,16].

15.4.1 Probability Density Function

GMM works on the assumption that the data points are generated by a mixture of K Gaussian distributions, where K is a user-specified value. A cluster in the data is represented by a Gaussian distribution, and the GMM model calculates the probability that each data point belongs to each cluster. Blending coefficients, which represent the proportions of each Gaussian in the mixture, are also estimated by the model.

The probability density function of the GMM model is the sum of the probability density functions of each Gaussian distribution, weighted by the associated mixing coefficients. The GMM model can be represented mathematically as follows:

[N(x;_i,_i)p(x)=_iK w_i*N(x;_i,_i)] (15.1)

where {p(x)} is the GMM model's probability density function, {w_i} is the i-th Gaussian's mixing coefficient, {N(x; _i, _i)} is the i-th Gaussian's probability density function with mean{_i} and covariance matrix {_i}, and (K) is the number of Gaussians in the mixture. The sum of all the mixing coefficients is equal to one, i.e., [Σ_i^K w_i = 1].

15.4.2 Training the GMM

Expectation-maximization (EM) algorithms are commonly used to train GMM models. It is an iterative algorithm between computing the posterior probabilities of data points belonging to each cluster (the E step) and updating the changing parameters of the Gaussian and mixture coefficients (M step). The EM method starts with initial estimates of the Gaussian and mixture coefficient parameters and iteratively tunes the parameters until convergence.

15.4.3 Expectation Stage

Step E of the EM algorithm computes the posterior probabilities of the data points belonging to each cluster, based on the current parameter estimates. The following probability of data point x belonging to the i-th cluster is given by Bayes rule:

γ_i(x) = w_i * N(x;_i,_i) /p(x) (15.2)

where {p(x)} is the information point's generally likelihood thickness work and {_i(x)} is the back likelihood of x having a place to the i-th cluster. The back probabilities are at that point utilized to overhaul the blending coefficient estimations.

15.4.4 Maximization Stage

Given current estimates of the following probabilities, step M of the EM method updates the estimates of the Gaussian parameter and the mixing coefficient. The mean value of the i-th Gaussian is updated as follows:

[μ_i = ∑_j ∧ n γ_i(x_j) * x_j/∑_j ∧ n γ_i(x_j)] (15.3)

where n is the total number of data points, and x_j is the j-th data point.

The covariance matrix of the i-th Gaussian is updated as:

[Σ_i = Σ_j ∧ n γ_i(x_j) * (x_j − μ_i) * (x_j − μ_i) ∧ T/Σ_j ∧ n γ_i(xj)]. (15.4)

The mixing coefficient of the i-th Gaussian is updated as:

[w_i = ∑_j ∧ n γ_i(x_j)/n]. (15.5)

15.4.5 Applications of GMM

15.4.5.1 Clustering

Clustering is an application of GMM. Clustering is the method of separating a dataset into bunches or clusters depending on how comparative the information focuses are. By demonstrating the dataset as a blend of Gaussian conveyances and evaluating the parameters of the blend demonstrate to bunch comparable information focuses, GMM may be utilized for clustering. The number of clusters or components within the blend is frequently decided in development in GMM-based clustering. The GMM demonstrates at that point apportions each information point to one of the components depending on the probability that it has a place to that component. Each information point is allotted to the component with the most noteworthy probability to deliver the clustering result.

15.4.5.2 Density Estimation

One of the most important applications of GMM is density estimation. The technique of determining the underlying probability density function of a data set is called density estimation. By modeling the data set as a mixture of Gaussian distributions, GMM can be used to estimate the density of the data set. The probability density function of a data set is represented in GMM as the weighted sum of the Gaussian distributions, with each Gaussian distribution representing a component of the mixture. Each Gaussian component of the mixture has a mean, a covariance matrix, and a weight that determines its contribution to the total density function. Based on the input data set, the GMM model calculates these parameters. By evaluating the probability density function of the mixture at this point, the GMM model can be used to estimate the density of a new data point. The GMM model provides a probability estimate of the density function, which can be used to compute statistics such as the mean, variance, and higher order moment of a data set.

15.4.5.3 Data Generation

GMM may be utilized for information generation in expansion to thickness estimation. Unused information focuses can be made from a prepared GMM as shown by inspecting from the blend conveyance. The GMM demonstrated creates unused information focuses by beginning with selecting a component from the blend depending on its weight. This component is at that point utilized to build a unused information point by inspecting from the Gaussian dispersion that compares to it. The strategy is proceeded until a certain sum of information focuses are produced. The created information focuses will be factually comparable to the first dataset utilized to prepare the GMM model. However, since GMM may be a probabilistic show that makes data points based on the learned dispersion, the created information focuses are not culminate copies of the first dataset [11,16,17].

15.4.5.4 Advantages Of GMM

15.4.5.4.1 Flexibility

GMM may be a profoundly versatile demonstration able to capture a wide extent of dispersions, counting non-spherical clusters, covering clusters, and skewed conveyances. GMM's versatility makes it suitable for a wide extent of applications, including complicated information displaying in fund, science, and picture investigation.

15.4.5.4.2 Probability Estimate

GMM gives a probabilistic degree of information point closeness. This metric can be valuable in a assortment of applications, counting irregularity recognizable proof and exception examination, where measuring the degree of uniqueness from anticipated behavior is crucial.

15.4.5.4.3 Parameter Estimation

GMM can estimate mixed model parameters using either maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) or Bayesian methods. Even for datasets with large numbers of sizes, these methods can produce reliable parameter estimates [1].

15.4.5.4.4 Data Generation

GMM can create modern information focuses with factual highlights indistinguishable from the initial dataset. This is useful in an assortment of applications, counting information increase and reenactment, where it is a basic requirement to deliver new information focuses that are better than basic dissemination.

15.4.5.4.5 Interpretability

Since each component of the blend compares to a Gaussian conveyance with its claim cruel and covariance lattice, GMM produces interpretable results. This capability can be useful in a assortment of applications, such as picture division and content clustering, where the clustering comes about must be deciphered.

15.4.5.4.6 Scalability

GMM is adaptable to expansive datasets and may be utilized in conjunction with other optimization procedures to extend the model's computing productivity, such as expectation-maximization (EM) and variational induction.

15.4.5.5 Limitations

15.4.5.5.1 Initialization

GMM can be sensitive to parameter initialization, resulting in inferior clustering results. If the initialization is insufficient, the algorithm may become trapped in local optima.

15.4.5.5.2 Number Of Components

The sum of fixings within the combination must be set ahead of time. Choosing the perfect number of components may be troublesome, especially for high-dimensional datasets, and may require a huge sum of trial and error.

15.4.5.5.3 Computationally Expensive

GMM may be computationally demanding, especially for huge datasets. A few parameters, such as implies, covariances, and weights, must be evaluated by this strategy, which can be time-consuming for huge datasets.

15.4.5.5.4 Curse Of Dimensionality:

The reviled dimensionality, in which the number of parameters vital to legitimately depict the dispersion develops exponentially with the number of measurements, might influence GMM. This could result in overfitting or destitute execution, particularly with expansive datasets.

15.4.5.5.5 Overlapping Clusters:

GMM results in the suspicion that clusters are frequently disseminated and non-overlapping, which may not be genuine for all datasets. Covering clusters can create befuddling discoveries and make assessing clustering comes about challenging.

15.4.5.5.6 Sensitivity To Outliers:

Exceptions within the dataset might cause GMM to be touchy, influencing parameter estimation and clustering results. To decrease the impact of exceptions on the comes about, vigorous approaches such as trimmed probability estimation can be connected.

15.4.5.5.7 Interpretability:

Whereas GMM produces interpretable discoveries, it may be troublesome to translate for datasets with a high number of components or measurements. It can be challenging to discover important clusters and decipher the information in an important way.

15.6 Input Dataset

The data set is a collection of unbiased related raw data used to extract information, recognize patterns, analyze, make predictions, etc.

15.6.1 Types of Datasets

There are different types of datasets.

  • Cross-sectional data set,
  • Longitudinal dataset,
  • Time-series dataset,
  • Panel dataset,
  • Text dataset,
  • Image dataset,
  • Audio dataset,
  • video dataset, etc.

15.6.2 Properties of a Dataset

The primary properties of a dataset are.

  • Data should be unbiased,
  • Quality of data should be good,
  • There should be low noise in the data,
  • Size of data should be large enough to analyze,
  • Data should represent the population.
  • The quantity of outliers should be low.

There are also more properties of a data set.

15.6.3 Introduction of Kaggle

Kaggle is one of the most popular and trusted websites for Data Science, Machine learning, and Artificial intelligence field. It hosts competitions for engineers and IT professionals. Kaggle is a reliable source of many kinds of data as many world-famous organizations and companies post data on it. In this project we will use “Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset” which is freely available in Kaggle.com. Dataset Link: https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/vbookshelf/rice-leaf-diseases

15.6.4 Why Did We Choose “Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset”?

Rice is by far the most significant food crop for people in low- and lower-middle-income nations out of the three primary crops—wheat, maize, and rice. In low-income nations, both the affluent and the poor eat rice, but the poorest individuals consume comparatively little wheat, making them particularly sensitive to the price and availability of rice.

Rice is a fundamental and typically indispensable food staple in many Asian nations, especially for the poor. Rice makes up about half of the food costs and, on average, a fifth of household expenditures for the poor people in Asia who make less than $1.25 per day. This one group buys rice every year for the equivalent of $62 billion (in purchasing power parity). Many of the world's poor people depend on rice for their food security.

Over half of the world's population relies on rice as a staple food, making it one of the most significant crops worldwide. However, several things, including diseases, can have an impact on rice production. One of the most prevalent and harmful diseases that impact rice crops, leaf diseases result in considerable output losses globally. To discover and investigate these illnesses, researchers have produced databases, one of which is the Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset.

15.6.5 About the Dataset

Researchers from Gujrat, India's Dharmsinh Desai University assembled the dataset. To ensure that they were representative of the many illnesses that damage rice leaves, scientists carefully selected the photographs from rice fields in Gujrat. Using a high-resolution camera, the images were taken, and professional plant pathologists identified each disease type.

Table 15.1 shows the input dataset of spotted leaf images of rice leaves with the four prevalent leaf diseases Brown Spot, Bacterial Blight, and Leaf Smut, included in the Rice Leaf Diseases collection, a publicly accessible collection. There are 120 jpeg photos in the collection, and each one is labeled with the disease it portrays. The goal of this collection is to support the development of automated disease detection tools that can assist farmers in spotting and treating infections early on, hence reducing crop losses.

Table 15.1 Input Dataset of Spotted Leaf

Each of the 1,024 × 1,024-pixel photos in the collection were taken using a smartphone camera under natural lighting circumstances. Forty photos each of Brown Spots, Bacterial Blight, and Leaf Smut are included in the collection. The dataset's images are each labeled with the name of the disease they depict, making it simple for researchers to teach machine learning models to recognize the many leaf diseases.

15.8 Conclusion

In India, a tropical cultivation-focused nation, leaf diseases are a major cause for concern for vertical farming because they can significantly harm both productivity and the economy. In this study, a machine learning model based on a Gaussian mixture model was introduced to quickly and correctly diagnose rice leaf disease. The machine learning model performed effectively and efficiently after classifying a picture from the input dataset due to the sizeable dataset. This high-end strategy has demonstrated a societal impact on people's lives. Thus, it can be concluded that leaf diseases are a major issue for farmers all over the world and can have a big negative influence on yields and the economy. The accuracy, speed, and cost-effectiveness of conventional disease detection methods are constrained, but emerging technologies, such as molecular diagnostics and machine learning, hold the promise of more effective and efficient disease management methods.
