16 Multi-Objective Optimization of Economic Development and Environmental Issues in the Yangtze River Basin, China

Minh Thu Nguyen

Vietnam Institute of Meteorology, Hydrology and Climate Change, Hanoi, Vietnam

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-16

16.1 Environmental Issues and Non-Point Source Pollution

Pollutants are regarded as a harmful substance when they are discharged into the environment. Pollution in the environment include water pollution, soil pollution, and air pollution. However, NPS pollution is related to water pollution because they have non-identified sources and they cause pollution via the process of natural precipitation and snowmelt. These substances enter the aquatic environment and affect the environment's physical, chemical, and biological properties. In other words, water pollution is the release of polluting substances into surface water (lakes, streams, rivers, estuaries, and oceans) and groundwater, making water unsuitable for desired use aim or causing water quality impairment. Moreover, pollution of water resources comes normally from either point sources or non-point sources. Pollution of point source is often from power plants, sewage treatment plants, and oil wells (Harding et al. 1998; Wang et al. 2001). These sources are collected, identifiable, and are easy to be controlled as well as treated. However, pollution of NPS is from many different sources, such as land use in agriculture, forestry, urban and suburban development, mining, as well as others. Agricultural runoff accounts for a large proportion of NPS pollution because agricultural activities release a big number of contaminants including manure, fertilizers, pesticides, and eroded soil particles (David et al. 1997; Robinson 2005; Novotny 1982). Their loads are affected by surface runoff, interflow, and ground water base runoff from farms and agricultural land. The most bothersome contaminants are ammonia, nutrients, sediments, bacteria, pesticides, salt, selenium, and thermal modification. They impair water quality and cause disease for organisms like waterborne diseases. If a huge number of chemical fertilizers from agriculture are discharged into a water body, the excess nitrogen factor will go into ground water and streaming branches. Moreover, using pesticide in agriculture can cause it to enter water bodies and result in NPS pollution phenomenon.

NPS pollution relates to human activities and natural factors from excess use of fertilizer as well as pesticide in agricultural areas or from toxic chemicals from urban runoffs, etc. The EPA estimated that NPS pollution from agriculture activities accounts for 60% in impairing and threatening surface water. Subsequently, it can drive property values down (Shen et al. 2012; EPA 2003) because runoffs pick polluting substances up and carry them away in a natural way, and deposit them into groundwater, inland water, coastal water, and streaming systems. NPS pollution can affect community and human health, such as the scenic allure and health of surface water (Ding et al. 2010), or kill aquatic organisms due to impairment of oxygen concentration in the water. Thus, affective level of non-point source pollution can cause more serious harm to human health and the environment from water quality impairment due to eutrophication and it is more difficult to control/treat because of its non-identified release sources.

16.2 Economic Development and Non-Point Source Pollution in China

Around 1978, China began to open up to the world. In the past four decades, China has been successful in its reform process via eliminating extreme poverty and transforming its economy. The country's sustained growth has created more opportunities to integrate with the world's economy and presently more than 800 million of its people have lifted themselves out of poverty. This economic growth has created a historic transition for China's people from an agricultural economy to an urban economy. This transition has allowed 260 million people to move from working in agriculture to productive economic activities in big cities [WB 2021]. Today, China's people are at high middle-income level and China will become a high-income developed country in the future. This is a key goal in China's national development policy.

The real issue of environmental pollution in China is demonstrated by its population growth and economic development. In the past few decades, China's economy has developed quickly and it has come to be regarded as the world's factory. Many countries have since invested in China. China's industrialization and developed economy has helped its people to improve their lives as well as raise GDP growth. According to World Bank data, China's average GDP growth has been over 9% per year [WB 2019]. However, a rapidly developing economy combined with population growth has resulted in extreme pressure on its water resources and caused environmental pollution in China. Water shortages and pollution occur in many localities. Although China is not the main source of historical cumulative emissions, today the country accounts for 27% of annual global carbon dioxide release and a third of the world's greenhouse gas emissions [WB 2014] and its air as well as water pollution affects China's people and other countries. Thus, China's rapid growth is alongside environmental problems and leading to an imbalance between economic development and environmental protection. This has caused a strain on sustainable growth.

Non-point source pollution also is being paid attention in China because it is really a difficult issue to control and manage well. NPS pollution normally comes from various sources with complex spatial distribution. In 1990 about 30%–50% waterbodies were polluted in China by NPS pollution. Many NPS pollution sources are from agricultural activities such as use of pesticides and fertilizers, which are not controlled leading to excessive concentrations of residues in soil. Additionally, erosion from land leads to increased sedimentation in river systems. Sediment is a suspended component, which can be transferred a far distance in a short time and suspended solids in water bodies make it difficult for aquatic organisms to absorb sunlight. Accumulation of nutrient substances with high concentration in water bodies leads to reduced dissolved oxygen, loss of biodiversity, algal bloom, and eutrophication. These affect aquatic organisms and their living environment, upset the balance of aquatic ecosystems, and cause danger to human health.

Over the past 30 years, China's economy has seen strong growth. Industrialization and modernization have become the hallmarks of the country. However, non-point source pollution issues have become longstanding problems that cannot be solved quickly because water bodies need a long time to recover. Therefore, pollutants accumulate slowly in water resources, the environment and affect human health. The Yangtze River basin is a key economic area that serves 185 towns in China and its population accounts for one-third of the country's population. The economy of the Yangtze River basin is focused on agricultural production. In March 2023, this area was a beneficiary of a program from the World Bank called Yangtze River Protection and Ecological Restoration that aimed to enhance ecological protection and reduce water pollution along the river basin. However, the results are not yet visible; pollution still exists in the Yangtze River basin. Whether there exists an optimal solution between economic development and environmental pollution for Yangtze River basin, is something that we will consider in the next section.

16.3 Urban Growth and Multiple Objective Optimizations

Urban growth is accompanied by industrialization and economic development (Walker 2001). Urbanization has allowed China's people more opportunities for working and moving from rural to urban area. Efficient urbanization can help China to best use its productive resources of people, land, and capital. It increases the welfare of people and results in more growth. Rapid urbanization has taken place in China during the past 30 years and it can be called a perfect growth model. With GDP increases, China today is among the largest economy of the world and its economy is second only to the United States. It is also the world's largest manufacturer. However, in parallel with these achievements, urban growth has resulted in many negative effects such as impairment of water quality and water pollution. Moreover, deterioration of water quality has been associated with urban growth because emission and pollution are not controlled. Nowadays, green urban growth is becoming a priority worldwide and in China too. Green economic growth is when a national economy develops/grows but it still ensures environmental issues are taken care of. At the core of green growth is GDP growth but minimum emission intensity. Thus, the question is how can positive growth be maintained while containing its negative impacts on the environment. An optimal model can solve this problem with a balance between the different objectives.

Factually, optimization theory has been applied in urban growth since the 1960s and is applied in different fields of science such as engineering, economics and logistics. Optimization can occur with two objectives or multi-objectives. For instance, if urban growth sees conflicts between different land-use groups, the optimization will become a multi-objective problem that needs optimal decisions to achieve a tradeoff between two or more conflicting objectives. The tradeoff exists between the objectives via the Pareto optimal solution. The Pareto front contains a set of non-dominated solutions and are feasible solutions. It moves from one feasible solution to another, which moves at least one objective function to a better value. Moreover, a combination between genetic algorithms in the optimal process allows for exact solutions in optimization. Genetic Algorithm (GA) is based on selection of natural genetics and survival of the fittest. It is found in different sciences, such as computational science, mathematics, economics and many other fields. Initially, a lot of individual solutions is made randomly in order to create a population. The number and size of population depend on each different issue. However, the number of a population usually contains several hundreds or thousands of feasible solutions. A next generation concludes a new population via a selection process. Certain selection results in more best solutions and a new generation is formed. From here, a population of solutions can be offered via Pareto optimization.

Urban growth in China has improved people's lives and also makes it difficult to control pollution because of rapid development as well as defects in treatment technology. In the Yangtze River Basin the non-point source pollution phenomenon is from an excess of nutrient substances from different sources, particularly from agricultural activities. This pollution not only affects the environmental landscape, but also influences living organisms. In order to control and manage the flow of these nutrients into the Yangtze River basin as well as protect the basin's water quality, a multi-objective optimization of genetic algorithm is researched based on GDP objective, changing cost objective, compatibility objective between land use, and NPS pollution load objective to find the optimization between urban growth and environmental pollution. In particular, optimal solutions of two-dimensional genetic algorithm are combined with multiple objectives:

  1. Maximization of GDP value
  2. Minimization of NPS pollutant load
  3. Minimization of the cost of changing from the status quo
  4. Minimization of incompatibility between the land uses

And constraints:

  1. Urban construction land > a certain level
  2. Agricultural land > a certain level
  3. Water bodies unchanged

16.4 Objective Evaluation

16.4.1 Maximization of GDP Value

Maximization of economic benefit is calculated as:

$MAX\left( Z_{1} \right) = \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{n}{Unit\_ GDP_{InbasinAd\_ i} \ast A_{i}}$ (16.1)

Where:

  1. Z1: Total GDP value in the basin;
  2. Unit_GDPInbasinAd_i: GDP unit for the i-n land-use (RMB/m2);
  3. Ai: Total area on the i-th land-use (m2);

In order to calculate GDP value for each land-use type (agricultural land, wetland, desert land, urban construction land, grassland, water area, forest land, and barren land), the research used weighted method. Firstly, the research used the GDP value of urban fields from the statistical yearbook for the Yangtze River basin. Then, rate of area as administrative scope which belongs to the basin via equation:

$GDP_{InbasinAd\_ j} = GDP_{Ad_{j}} \ast \frac{AreaInbasinAd_{j}}{AreaAd_{j}}$ (16.2)

In which:

  1. AreaInbasinAdj is administrative scope;
  2. AreaAdj: GDP value is extracted for the basin,

Next, GDPInbasinAd_i and land-use area are extracted from raster data; GDP value is made on each i-th land-use kind in the j-th administrative scope. From that, Unit_GDPInbasinAd_i,j, can be calculated by the following equation as:

Unit_GDPInbasinAd_i, j = GDPInbasinAd_i, j/AreaInbasinAdi, j (16.3)

Weighting method is used and Unit_GDPInbasinAd_i is calculated as:

$Unit\_ GDP_{InBasinAd\_ i} = \sum\limits_{j = 1}^{19}{Unit\_ GDP_{InBasinAd\_ i,j} \ast \frac{AreaInBasinAd_{j}}{AreaBasin}}$ (16.4)

Where: Unit_GDPInbasinAd_i is the GDP value for each i-th land-use kind and 19 is the number of provinces on the Yangtze River Bain.

16.4.2 Minimization of NPS Pollutant Load

To estimate NPS pollutant load, a relationship between land-use types and NPS pollutant load is established, the export coefficient model (ECM) is used again in the optimization of urban growth. Therefore, the diminishing to minimum level of NPS pollution is the desired natural environment protection:

$MIN\left( Z_{2} \right) = \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{n}{\alpha\beta E_{i}A_{i}}$ (16.5)

Where:

  1. Z2: is pollutant load in the basin;
  2. Ei (ton/km2) is the export coefficient for i-th land use;
  3. Ai: Area of each land-use type (km2);
  4. α: precipitation impact factor
  5. β: terrain impact factor

With export coefficient Ei for i-th land-use type extracted in different patterns (8 types of land-use) for the Yangtze River Basin from many studies on NPS pollution by ECM (Ding et al. 2010; Liu et al. 2009); Precipitation impact factor and the terrain impact factor are identified via rate of precipitation and slope at location (i,j). They are calculated as:

$\alpha_{x,y} = \frac{{Precipation}_{x,y}}{averagePre}$ (16.6)

$\beta_{x,y} = \frac{{Slope}_{x,y}}{averageSlo}$ (16.7)

Where: (x,y) is the location of the cell; Precipationx,y and Slopex,y are the precipitation and slope at location (x,y), respectively; Average Pre and average Slo are average precipitation and average slope.

16.4.3 Minimization of Changing Cost Index

Changing cost index is calculated via dimensionless value from i-th land use to j-th land-use because dimensionless cost can present the relationship of different land-use changing and approaching nearly its real cost (Zhang et al. 2010). It is presented as:

$MIN(Z_{3}) = \sum\limits_{(x,y) \in \cup}{{change}\_{index}_{(x,y)}}$ (16.8)

Where:

  1. Z3: Cost value in land-use changing;
  2. U: Basin area;
  3. (x,y): Land-use cell in the basin;

In which, change_index(x,y) is the changing cost index for land-use cell (x,y), and change_index(x,y) is identified via current land-use situation and the optimized land-use for land use cell (x,y). It ranges from 0–1, and with higher value will get higher cost in land-use changing. This changing cost index is referenced from existing research (Balling et al. 2004).

16.4.4 Minimization of Incompatibility Index

Incompatibility is presented via total incompatibility index of center land-use cell and neighboring cells including 9 cells and is identified by review materials (Ligmann et al. 2008; Cao et al. 2012). This index ranges from 0–1. A higher value reveals more big conflict happening between land-use types. Calculation is showed as:

$MIN(Z_{4}) = \sum\limits_{(x,y) \in U}{\sum\limits_{({ii},{jj}) \in N_{x,y}}{{IncompleteIndex}({Landuse}(x,y),{Landuse}(ii,jj))}}$ (16.9)

Where:

  1. Z4: incompatibility value;
  2. U: Basin area;
  3. Nx,y: 8 neighboring cells of center cell (x,y);
  4. Landuse(x,y): Land-use type for cell (x,y), and Land-use (ii,jj) is represented for land-use type of cell (ii,jj);
  5. Incomplete Index (Land-use (x,y) and Land-use (ii,jj)): incompatibility index between Landuse (x,y) and Landuse (ii,jj).

16.4.5 Constraint 1: Urban Construction Land

Based on the population in 2010 and area value of per capita urban construction land to make a statistical population data in the basin. From that finding of annual growth rate and using this statistical population data to prove urban construction land in the basin, which is no less than a certain value.

16.4.6 Constraint 2: Agricultural Land

According to Regulations on Basic farmland Protection, of total agricultural land area in 2010, agricultural land in the basin was more than 80% of total land area. Therefore, total agricultural land is not less than a certain value.

16.4.7 Constraint 3: Water Bodies

In the basin, except for the main river flow, many branches and lakes supply/enter the Yangtze River basin. It is very important to the water ecological system and in order to conduct this research, the water space should not be occupied by urban development process.

16.4.8 Genetic Algorithm Process

Multiple objectives can be combined with one-dimensional genetic algorithm (GA) or two-dimensional GA. Normally, GA for one-dimensional is applied in land-use when it is divided similar to traffic lines. This significant trait makes it easy to identify and assess non-spatial objectives. However, with spatial objectives, it is difficult to apply one-dimensional GA because there is a gap between two land-use type and neighbor cells. Therefore, applying two-dimensional GA will resolve this problem and it becomes easier to optimize urban growth, in which land-use grid in a raster map is denoted by genes. Each gene is set up as an integer and is ranged from 1 to th-land-use number. A genetic algorithm is started by 100 random land-use patterns and is presented as a generation, in which constraints and satisfying are ranked by fitness. The next generation is the second 100 land-use patterns and it is gained via selection; crossover; mutation; and elitism process. Such a process takes place until the last generation. The processes of two-dimensional genetic algorithm are revealed as:

  1. Selection: from the set of solutions, a number of fitter individuals are selected and their name is called as mother and father.
  2. Crossover: offspring from more than one parent is selected, then one or more offsprings are produced via crossover and they use the genetic material of the parents.
  3. Mutation: the mutation happens in natural genetics when the children have features that are not the same as their parents and this process is conducted to avoid a local optimum.
  4. Elitism: to gain the best solution in each generation, the first solutions that have the highest fitness value will be transferred to next generation without any operation.

16.5 Optimization of the Objectives

16.5.1 Optimization of Non-Point Source Load and Changing Cost Index

From two-dimensional GA for optimizing, the research proposes optimal solutions of non-point source load and other objectives, in which these optimal solutions satisfy the objective functions although conflicts occur between objectives.

Figure 16.2 presents optimal solutions between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives with condition of objectives being minimization of NPS pollution load and minimization of changing cost index objectives. In fact, it is very difficult for a relationship to exist between two these objectives because to keep NPS pollution to a minimum value, a high cost must be paid for treatment and controlling it. Nevertheless, via the optimal process of two-dimensional genetic algorithm, the research indicates the existence of two feasible solution sets for the first generation and the last generation between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives.

In the first generation, the research finds four optimal solutions and two weakly feasible points belonging to criterion space. They are laid on the Pareto frontier. However, they only have four optimal solutions and are regarded as a set of optimal solutions with the best value as red Pareto front line, which satisfy conditions on minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index objectives. Thus, this optimal solution set gains the tradeoff of objectives by minimum changing cost index and it still ensures no pollution from non-point source. Optimization of this first generation also indicates two weak optimal solutions. They are not regarded as the best solutions belonging to the set of optimal solutions because they only satisfy minimization of non-point source load when changing cost index is high. The set only satisfies one objective while optimal condition is satisfied on two objectives. Moreover, the research shows also Pareto frontier in the second rank including 12 optimal solutions, which are non-dominated individuals in the population than the first rank. However, the second rank has more optimal points than the first rank and the individuals that lie below space are more optimal than above space of the second rank frontier. The individuals are located further on the Pareto frontier, which has diminishing optimization between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives in the criterion space.

Figure 16.3 is a population including individuals and is achieved after two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimizing process. The last generation of optimal process for non-point source load and changing cost index objectives indicates that the best solutions in this generation is more than the first generation as 2 optimal solutions. These six optimal solutions make a set of optimal points with the best value and they satisfy objective conditions of minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index. The last generation especially does not appear to show a weakly optimal solution. Thus, in the last generation exists a perfect Pareto set with six optimal points that lie on the red Pareto frontier, and gains the tradeoff between objectives. Furthermore, the research finds also six optimal points in the second rank with optimal value less than the first rank Pareto frontier. Therefore, in multi-objective optimization of non-point source load and changing cost index we present four optimal solutions in the first generation and six optimal solutions in the last generation as Pareto frontiers that satisfy the tradeoff between objectives.

16.5.2 Optimization of Non-Point Source Load and GDP

When considering objectives in maximization of GDP and minimization of non-point source load, conflicts are revealed between the objectives. These objectives have high conflicts because a country with higher GDP and development has people with high living standards; high consumption; increased pollution emission; as well needing more urban construction areas to develop and produce. This development leads to increased pollution in the environment, especially in emission of non-point sources. However, people are now directing their energy in national development to mitigate environment pollution while still ensuring economic development. Thus, there is conflict between these objectives but optimal solutions can be be found. They are demonstrated by two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization process in this research via minimization of NPS pollution load and maximization of GDP objectives. Figure 16.4 is the optimal map of two these objectives in the first generation and the last generation, where individuals make up a population and are located in the criterion space. The map indicates that in the first generation, a set of optimal points includes five of the best solutions that lie on red Pareto frontier and located on the left of the criterion space. These optimal solutions have the best value and satisfy both maximization of GDP and minimization of non-point source load as well as achieving the tradeoff between objectives. This is a perfect Pareto set. Furthermore, the research proposes eight optimal points in the second rank that are non-dominated than optimal solutions in the first rank. However, optimal solutions in the first rank are less than the second rank in the criterion space.

After the selection process, crossover, mutation, and elitism of individuals in two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization, the last generation of two these objectives reveal a set of feasible decision space, which includes 12 optimal solutions located on the left of the criterion space as red Pareto frontier. Figure 16.5 shows that the number of Pareto optimal solutions in the last generation is more than the first generation as seven best solutions and of course they satisfy conditions on maximization of GDP with minimization of non-point source load, while still achieving the tradeoff of objectives. Moreover, in the second rank of optimal solutions, the research indicates there are 15 optimal points, which are non-dominated individuals than the first rank. Thus, 12 optimal points is the best solution of Pareto optimization set in the last generation for GDP and non-point source load objectives. Therefore, multi-objective optimization of non-point source load minimization and maximization of GDP objectives indicates 5 the best solutions in the first generation and 12 optimal solutions in the last generation. They lie on the red Pareto frontier and gain the tradeoff between objectives.

16.5.3 Optimization of NPS Load and Land Use Incompatibility Index

Land use compatibility is understood as attaining the highest and best in land-use. Land-use compatibility can reduce the conflicts between neighboring land-uses and result in less change around land use areas. Moreover, land use compatibility is also regarded as an index to assess conflicting land uses about economy and society in community. In particular, it has become more important in urban development, and minimization of land use incompatibility means maximum diminishing of conflicts in land use. If compared to non-point source load, this means something is not factual because urban development results in increasing and expanding land uses leading to a rise in conflicting land uses and also emits more pollution. Thus, minimization of land use incompatibility and minimization of non-point source load are difficult to find in reality because they are highly conflicted with each other. Nevertheless, selecting individuals and genetic algorithm optimal process propose two sets of optimal solutions for the first generation and the last generation for non-point source load and land use incompatibility index objectives. Figure 16.5 indicates those optimal solution sets.

In the first generation, a set of Pareto optimal points is located on the left of the criterion space including four optimal points with the best solutions. They are laid on the red Pareto frontier and satisfy optimal conditions of minimization of land use incompatibility and minimization of non-point source load objectives.

Moreover, via optimal process, two weak optimal points are found lying on the red Pareto front line which only satisfy minimization of non-point source load but do not satisfy minimization of land use incompatibility index. Except for the best optimal solutions, the research also indicates the existence of non-dominated individuals in the second rank including 11 optimal points whose values are less than optimal than the first rank, which is black Pareto frontier. Thus, the first generation of above objectives proposes four optimal solutions as a set of Pareto optimization.

In the last generation of multiple objectives of optimization with minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives, the research indicates a set of feasible solutions concluding six optimal points that satisfy the conditions of objectives of minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load and form a red Pareto front line. Moreover, the figure also shows there are three weak optimal solutions lying on the blue Pareto frontier too of the first rank. They are not regarded as the best solutions because they only satisfy the condition of one objective of minimization of non-point source load but they have high land use incompatibility index, as well as gaining the tradeoff of objectives. Furthermore, the research presents also optimal points in the second rank with ten feasible solutions, which include more non-dominated individuals than ones in the first rank. From ten of these individuals, there are five individuals that satisfy secondary optimization solution. The remaining individuals can be regarded as very weak optimal points because they only satisfy minimization of non-point source load objective (five optimal points) or minimization of land use incompatibility index objective. Thus, in the last generation of these objectives there exists six optimal solutions with the best value which are shown on the black Pareto frontier. The optimization process on minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives denotes four optimal solutions in the first generation and six best solutions in the last generation. They are presented by the Pareto frontier and reveals as a perfect Pareto set.

16.6 Summary

Generally, multi-objective optimization is the tradeoff between objectives. Via two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimal process for minimization of NPS pollution load, minimization of changing cost index, maximization of GDP value, and minimization of incompatibility index objectives, this research indicates that although these objectives are conflicting with each other, optimal solutions still exist between them, which create the tradeoff of objectives. In particular, there are four optimal solutions in the first generation and six optimal solutions in the last generation in minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index objectives; five best solutions in the first generation and 12 optimal solutions in the last generation in minimization of non-point source load and maximization of GDP value objectives; four optimal solutions in the first generation and six best solutions in the last generation in minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives. They are laid on the red Pareto frontier and revealed as perfect Pareto sets.

17 Qualitative Study on E-Commerce and Brick-and-Mortar Stores A Machine Learning Approach

Sanjay Das

Research Scholar, Department of Management and Marketing, West Bengal State University, Barasat, North 24 Parganas, West Bengal, India

Soumen Santra

Assistant Professor, Department of MCA, Techno International New Town, Chakpachuria, West Bengal, India

Anirban Sarkar

Professor and Head, Department of Management and Marketing, West Bengal State University, Barasat, North 24 Parganas, West Bengal, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-17

17.1 Introduction

Electronic commerce (e-commerce) refers to digitally enabled commercial transactions between and among organizations and individuals (Laudon and Traver 2018). Brick and Mortar stores have been used by all customers before e-commerce platforms became available.

With the spread and popularity of the internet, together with the availability of smartphones at an affordable price, a larger proportion of the Indian population got the opportunity to use e-commerce. With the benefits offered by the combination of the internet and smartphone, e-commerce has been adopted by a growing number of customers and marketers. In 2022, the Indian e-commerce market was predicted to increase by 21.5%, reaching US$ 74.8 billion i.e., Rs.5,98,400 crores. The Indian e-commerce market is expected to grow to Rs 16, 20,000 crores (US$ 200 billion) by 2026 (IBEF 2022). For the 2021 festive season, Indian e-commerce platforms generated sales with a Gross Merchandise Value (GMV) of US$ 9.2 billion i.e., Rs.73,600 crore, a 23% increase from the previous year's US$ 7.4 billion. This trend indicates that more and more customers are adopting e-commerce to fulfill their purchase requirements.

With the adoption of e-commerce, its comparison with B&M stores has become a subject of discussion among customers as they begin to notice the pros and cons of both. Due to this increasing popularity of online sales, the revenue of pure B&M retailers has been reducing gradually which is a warning for them (Dahiya 2017). Operators and stakeholders of B&M stores are becoming apprehensive about the future of retail business in B&M mode. Whether they would be able to survive at all with their pure B&M stores and if they survive, how long would they be able to continue become a pertinent question to many of them? To understand the factors behind the concern of the B&M retailers, a Direct Interview (DI) of B&M retailers was conducted followed by an analysis of the responses.

In this study, reasons behind customers’ preference for e-commerce in comparison with the reasons behind visiting B&M retailers have been captured through focus group discussion (FGD). FGD is used as a qualitative approach to gain an in-depth understanding of social issues when the aim is to obtain data from a purposely selected group of individuals rather than from a statistically representative sample of a broader population (O.Nyumba et al. 2018). Key reasons were identified as “Themes” using “Thematic Analysis” (TA) which is a method for identifying, analyzing, and interpreting patterns of meaning (“themes”) within qualitative data (Clarke and Braun 2016). NLP i.e., natural language processing (IBM Cloud Education 2020) has been used on the extract of FGD and DI to identify the features thereby validating the themes.

Selection between B&M and e-commerce depends on various factors (Themes). Based on the analysis done in this study recommendations for B&M retailers are mentioned in the conclusions.

17.2 Review of Literature

Clarke and Braun (2016) contended that thematic analysis (TA) is a method for identifying, analyzing, and interpreting patterns of meaning (“themes”) from qualitative data. They also mentioned that TA can be used to identify patterns within and across data obtained from the experience, views, and perspectives of the participants. According to the authors, TA can be used to analyze both large and small datasets (from 1 to 2 participants to large interview studies with 60 or more participants) of both homogenous and heterogeneous samples.

Customers’ attitudes toward the internet have changed significantly during the last two decades (Chaffey and Chadwick 2016). As a result, in consumer markets, customers are becoming more used to buying online and digital technologies enable them to be well-informed while deciding on purchases. As consumers are becoming more informed, the struggle for power between consumers and retailers will continue. Moreover, an increasingly important role is being played by social networks in linking consumers and potential business partners. This clearly states that the business environment is in favor of the e-commerce players and therefore, the B&M retailers should be aware and alert to initiate suitable actions to remain relevant.

Singla, Randhawa and Jain (2018) described customers' reliance on online reviews as positive or negative which has significantly expanded because of an evolutionary shift from offline to online markets. Online reviews have evolved into a tool for fostering confidence and influencing consumer purchasing behavior. With such dependence, it becomes necessary to manage a vast volume of reviews and provide the buyer with reliable reviews. Following data balancing with a nearly equal number of favorable and unfavorable ratings, three different classification models have been employed. The authors showed how machine learning models such as Bayes theorem, decision tree, support vector machine, etc. are getting high accuracy.

With technological advancements and ease of internet accessibility, it has become possible to conduct “Focus Group Discussion” using internet-based (Daniels et al. 2019) audio-visual software. Online platforms overcome any geographical limitations and enhance opportunities for real-time discussions in groups that otherwise might not be feasible. During this research “Focus Group Discussion” i.e., FGDs were conducted online with consumers used to buy online as well as from B&M retailers.

As the data emerging out of customers’ statements during the FGD is a pertinent source of information, data intensive approach deploying machine learning as mentioned by Peters et al. (2014) was considered during this research for information retrieval to support business decision-making. The author emphasized the approach to identify and filter data of mixed quality from disparate sources. Using machine learning algorithms, it is possible to fetch the intensive nature of data which is known as intensive data learning approach from the perspective of big data concepts.

Leelawat et al. (2022) showed that machine learning algorithms are capable of accurately predicting the goals and feelings of the source material. After categorizing the text into sentiment and intention classes, the word within each category can be used to determine the sentiments and travel-related intentions of readers. The authors have shown the process of word pulling and the method to find out the sentiment polarity from the word cloud. Based on the customer's status and the data set received from tweets, their sentiment about the journey specifications was found.

Vojvodić (2019) mentioned that advanced in-store technologies are available for B&M retailers which can be deployed to interact with customers. These interactive technologies can also provide insight into the information on trends in consumer behavior bringing out the details about their expectations, needs, and preferences. The author categorically mentioned that, despite the perceived diminishing role, B&M retailers have the potential to be powerful players in the future of shopping.

Industries have been highly affected by digitalization and have had to transform their business models because technology has been transforming consumer behavior and the relationship between consumers with businesses is getting reshaped (Gerea, Gonzalez-Lopez and Herskovic 2021). Therefore, it is expected that customers will also behave accordingly in this environment. To meet the requirement of the customers, many traditional retailers and service-based companies transformed themselves to omnichannel mode to respond to serve the customers to their satisfaction in the competitive market.

Omnichannel businesses have emerged due to extensive dissemination of digital technologies along with changing consumer behavior (Lehrer and Trenz 2022). The marketer can offer continuous and consistent customer experience over multiple digital and physical channels. Omnichannel businesses operate channels in an integrated fashion to get synergistic advantages and elevate the level of customer experience by offering the customers an opportunity to seamlessly move across digital and physical environments.

Omnichannel retailing aims to deliver a seamless shopping experience to customers by providing product information display, information search including comparison, and an online payment system (Thaichon et al. 2022). Retailers can thus redefine both in-store and online experiences of the customers in choosing a product by using the technology thereby improving the relationship quality.

17.3 Objectives of the Study

The objectives that emanate from the review of the literature are listed below:

  1. To have a theme-based comparison between e-commerce and B&M retailers from the perspective of customers who have exposure and experience with both systems.
  2. To understand from the B&M retailers the weakness and the threat as perceived by them with increasing acceptance of e-commerce along with their strength areas based on which they have served the customers.
  3. To identify a path for sustenance for the B&M stakeholders to remain relevant to prospects and customers based on their strengths discovered through “Thematic Analysis.”

17.4 Methods

The study was conducted in Kolkata deploying qualitative methods in three parts.

Focus Group Discussions (FGD) with customers: As an FGD must be conducted involving multiple participants who share common characteristics that are relevant from the research perspective, participants (Van Eeuwijk and Angehrn 2017) were carefully chosen to ensure that they are conversant with both B&M as well as an online purchase. To obtain a wider range of information two separate FGDs were conducted taking a different set of participants. Considering the difficulties of having face-to-face group discussions during the COVID period, online FGDs (Daniels et al. 2019) were conducted. From the proceedings of the discussion held in the group, the reasons that came out for choosing e-commerce, as well as brick-and-mortar, were identified as “Themes.” These themes were found to be further constituted of “Subthemes” which were the detailed supporting reasons under the main reason. Details of Themes and Subthemes as came out in FGDs are shown in Table 17.1.

Direct interviews (DI) of 14 B&M retailers of consumer electronic goods were conducted using a semi-structured questionnaire to understand their concerns about the prevailing market scenario. A purposive sampling technique was used for the selection of retailers. As many B&M retailers have developed their e-commerce channel also to become multi-channel (Savila, Wathoni and Santoso 2019), both types of retailers i.e., pure B&M and multichannel retailers have been covered while interviewing retailers. Key factors of the concern voiced out by the retailers were flagged as “Themes” which were supported by “Subthemes.” Details of Themes and Sub-themes as indicated by retailers have been listed in Table 17.2.

Natural Language Processing known as NLP (IBM Cloud Education 2020), a versatile wing of Artificial Intelligence (AI), has been used on the qualitatively processed text captured during the FGD of customers and interviews of retailers. Our study proposed a model (as shown in Figure 17.1) wherein:

  1. The corpuses from each sentence captured from the customers and retailers were determined using Natural Language Tool Kit (NLTK) (Mukherjee et al. 2018)
  2. The model vectorized each word from the statements of customers and retailers and evaluated the feature of each corpus (Wang and Hu 2021) using TensorFlow.
  3. Using the sentiment analysis (Yao 2019) approach in TensorFlow the hidden features were found from the extracted features of the corpuses.
  4. Evaluating the feature factors, our model generated a list of features to work on.
  5. These features have been matched and compared with the themes identified manually to arrive at the final list of themes that needs to be addressed by the stakeholders of B&M retailers.

After getting the features the model used Google Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) and data analytics tool panda (python data analysis library) to get the tensor value of each corpus i.e., the tensor value of each word selected as a feature. The whole data set was converted into a dataframe. Based on this dataframe the TPU applied vectorization method and evaluated the tensor value of each corpus of which top 14 values have been considered for matching with the “Themes” and “Sub-themes” found during FGD (of customers) and DI (of retailers).

17.5 Results and Discussion Outcomes

Focus Group Discussion (FGD) sessions indicated four “Themes” i.e., Price, Information, Convenience and Confidence as shown in Table 17.1. Emphasis was made by the speakers on two aspects of price i.e., transparency and comparability which have been shown as “Subtheme.” The third subtheme of price was found to be the “Offer.” Offers are placed before the customers in the form of discounts or special prices with a closely approaching timeline during different occasions. The release of such offers on regular basis creates an expectation in the minds of prospects who plans to purchase the desired products. Thus, the e-commerce retailer creates an invisible engagement with the customers even before they order and attempt to prevent them from going to the B&M retailers.

“Information,” the next “Theme” identified during FGD, is about useful details that are made available to customers before, during, and after the purchase. This encompasses the “Subthemes,” “variants and specifications,” “delivery status” and “reviews.” The details regarding product variants, specifications, and prices that are available from e-commerce sites are not available to the prospects from B&M stores. This came out clearly during FGD as a reason to visit e-commerce websites. Real-time information on delivery status available to the customers is also exclusive for e-commerce and pointed out to be useful by FGD participants. “Reviews” are shown as a “Subtheme” within the group of the theme “Information.” Prospects check reviews to get the perception and feedback of customers (who purchased the product earlier) before buying. As the information is available 24×7 without any human intervention it attracts prospects from all segments creating aspiring customers. Not only the online customers, but the prospects who are used to buying from B&M also visit the e-commerce sites for product information, prices, and reviews.

Convenience, the third “Theme” identified in FGD, is because of the wide range of payment methods from payment by credit/debit card, app-based payment, and cash on delivery. The flexible and customer-oriented delivery system of e-commerce is the reason for having delivery as a “Subtheme.”

It was mentioned during FGD that, the “Touch & Feel” of a product that is only available at the B&M stores instils confidence in the mind of the buyer and motivates them to visit the store even if the purchase is finally made online. It was also mentioned that customers not only value the personal touch that is there in a brick-and-mortar store, but this is also something the customers miss while using e-commerce. Guidance of the sales personnel in the stores towards the selection of the right product is also found to be playing a role in attracting even those customers to B&M who are familiar with online purchases. For some specific products e.g., medicine where the expiry date is critical some customers prefer B&M only. Thus “Confidence” evolved as the fourth “Theme” of FGD comprising “Touch & Feel,” “Personal touch & advice” and “Product type specific need” as “Subthemes.”

Based on the business approach of the retailers as perceived from the responses received in Direct interviews (DI), B&M retailers have been categorized into three types as below-

Aggressive: These are Multichannel / Omnichannel retailers operating in multiple locations spread across Kolkata and its suburb, they have created a culture of challenging e-commerce players in their organization which inspires the customer-facing employees to maximize conversion by offering anything the customer may need. out of the walk-in. These retailers believe in the strength of face-to-face human interface which is not there in the case of e-commerce.

Competitive: These are pure B&M retailers located as neighborhood stores or at the market where customers visit regularly. These stores have been regularly reviewing the products and services that they offer in comparison with the trends and needs of the market. They update the product and service categories to continue to offer something which keeps them relevant to their target segment. They are aware of the competition of e-commerce not to surrender but to remain competitive.

Worried: These are pure B&M retailers who are worried about their future. They are not planning to adopt technology-based tools to start any internet-based activity. They consider it an expensive proposition and they feel that they are not familiar with the web-based activity. They believe that such B&M stores of consumer electronic goods will have to close within the next few years.

Though the retailers belonged to different categories, their areas of concern with respect to competition from e-commerce could be grouped into three factors designated as “Theme.” They are Price, Convenience and Situation. Price has been pointed out by many brick-and-mortar retailers as the major concern area. According to “Worried” retailers, e-commerce operators break the Market Operating Price i.e., MOP (price at which a product is usually sold by the B&M retailers) which is the main reason for the customers to move away from B&M retailers.

Convenience is the next theme as identified during the DI of retailers. “Trust-based purchase” along with “Touch and feel” are among the “Sub-Themes” under “Convenience.” These factors are largely responsible for the walk-in that happens in a retail outlet. B&M retailers engage people to interact with customers to get the exact requirement and gain trust by meeting the requirements over a period. Trust has a major influence on offline loyalty (Savila, Wathoni and Santoso 2019). This has been categorically mentioned by a retailer during the interview by stating “People believe people.” The brick-and-mortar retailer offers the “Touch and Feel” of the product while giving a demonstration to the family members when they come together and helping them to decide collectively thereby offering them good family time and making shopping a pleasure. B&M stores provide services like installation, buyback, and installment payment due to which buying from B&M is convenient for many customers. Their service also includes meeting specific requirements of customers in a particular situation e.g., taking care of home delivery and urgency.

Themes evolved during the FGD, and DI are found to be interlinked through certain “Subthemes.” To draw focus on themes that will be used to take further action, an NLP based model has been used. Relationships among the themes across FGD and DI and their matching with the features indicated by the NLP-based model have been shown in the Relationship diagram in Figure 17.2. The diagram reveals that Price is the Theme that is common for FGD and DI. It is a parameter that prospects normally compare before purchasing. E-commerce operators break the Market Operating Price (MOP) i.e., the price at which a product is usually sold by the B&M retailers and attempts to attract customers. Online sellers provide information of different types including “Reviews.” Convenience is also a Theme that is common for FGD and DI. This is supported by Subthemes “Touch & Feel” along with “Personal touch & advice.” This is linked with the “Requirement” of customers which can change depending on the “Situation.”

Further analysis done using Google Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) and data analytics tool panda (python data analysis library) brought out the tensor value of each corpus out of which, tensor values of the top 14 pertinent words have been shown in Table 17.3. In this table these words have also been matched with the “Themes” and “Sub-themes” as found during FGD (of customers) and DI (of retailers) and mentioned in Tables 17.1 and 17.2 as well as the relationship diagram in Figure 17.2.

The bar graph in Figure 17.3 shows the order and comparison of the tensor values. Based on the order of the tensor values, the nature of the words and their impact on the sentence can be understood. As it is not possible to understand the inner meaning of all the words and their implication in customers’ mind, tensor, or vector of each word of the feedback was needed. This intensive study of data by going deeper into the tensor values has shown the importance of the “Themes” and the “Subthemes” obtained regarding the B&M and e-commerce business through FGD and DI based process of the qualitative research.

17.6 Conclusion

Price has been an area of competition where e-commerce players are likely to be in advantageous position due to various reasons including economies of scale and logistics. B&M retailers need to apply their top strengths which include “Touch & Feel” facility of products and “Personal Touch” of their people and available at their stores. But these strengths can only be put to action if the customer comes to the store. This can be done by creating a website as first step of digitalization (Hokkanen, Walker and Donnelly 2020) to provide information about the products available and the services offered. Information should be made available online to customers in such a way that they are motivated to visit the retail stores with friends and family. The move that is needed is more for the mindset as compared to the process reengineering that is needed. It can be a dedicated website for the store or a common website for the market where multiple B&M stores can be present making the venture cost effective. B&M retailers having different types of product portfolios can use a common website for operational advantages. The approach should be Online to Offline (O2O) or vice versa providing the advantages of rich online information, online payment, best online price, offline physical experience and personalized customer service (Yang et al. 2020). Enterprising B&M retailers can go ahead and start offering online ordering that provides online sales followed by offline delivery. Delivering product at customers place is a known operation for retailers. After starting online sales with offline delivery, the B&M retailer can start need based door delivery in phased manner. They may not have the resources to offer everything on their own, but they can use services of third-party platforms (He, Gupta and Mirchandani 2021). The website will open a communication channel through which promotional input can be given to the customers in a cost-effective manner. It has been found that, customers react positively when retailers offer promotions that matches their shopping objective and the customers’ satisfaction level gets enhanced (Blom, Lange and Hess 2021).

For any requirement, everyone reaches out to the individuals and organization of confidence. The B&M retailer needs to keep track of customers’ requirements and cater accordingly to remain as a preferred supplier for them. “Touch & Feel” and “Personal Touch” of human beings are the potential winning tools which the B&M retailers should deploy consistently. Thus, the B&M retailers can migrate to a new way of doing business.

18 Design of Novel Energy Management System in Solar PV Powered EV Charging Station Using Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimization

M. Sathesh

Assistant Professor, Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Vel Tech Rangarajan Dr. Sagunthala R&D Institute of Science and Technology, Avadi, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Banu Priya Prathaban and R. Priyanka

Assistant Professor, Department of Networking and Communications, School of Computing, SRM Institute of Science and Technology, SRM Nagar, Kattankulathur, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: 10.1201/9781032725444-18

18.1 Introduction

In order to monitor electricity use and achieve energy savings, utilities and end users have both used Energy Management Systems (EMS). Along with assessing current systems and appliances, the methodology also takes into account the growing use of EMS in the most recent smart grids [1,2]. These grids reduce manpower usage by implementing smart meters in the household, but they require security.

The ideal conditions for power plants vary depending on their efficiency and power. The present type is effective up to a 150 °C separator temperature. The high pressure is influenced by the turbine concentration. Low collector cost is produced by high solar beam radiation and optimal cycle conditions [3,4].

18.2 Energy Management System (EMS) Implementation Setup

18.2.1 Solar PV Generation Monitoring

The EMS monitors the real-time solar PV generation capacity, including the energy produced by the solar panels and the current weather conditions. This data is used to determine the available solar energy for charging EVs [5].

18.2.2 Load Monitoring

The EMS tracks the power demand from the EV charging stations, considering the number of connected vehicles, their charging rates, and the charging schedules. This information helps determine the load requirements of the charging station [6].

18.2.3 Battery Storage Integration

The EMS is in charge of monitoring the batteries' state of charge (SOC) if the charging station includes battery energy storage. It maximizes the use of solar energy by optimizing battery charging and discharging, and it provides effective power management during times of high demand [7].

18.2.4 Demand Response

The EMS can take part in demand response programs in cases when the charging station is wired into the grid. It enables the charging station to alter its power consumption in response to grid signals, grid conditions, or pricing signals. This aids in grid stabilization and cost control for electricity [8].

18.2.5 Optimal Charging Scheduling

The EMS optimizes the charging schedules of the EVs based on various factors such as the availability of solar energy, EV owners' preferences, and grid conditions. It aims to minimize grid impact, maximize the utilization of solar energy, and ensure efficient charging for all connected vehicles [9].

18.2.6 Grid Integration and Balancing

The EMS ensures grid integration by monitoring the grid conditions, including voltage levels, frequency, and grid stability. It adjusts the charging station's operation to maintain a stable grid connection, preventing power quality issues and grid overloading [10].

18.2.7 Energy Monitoring and Reporting

The EMS collects and analyzes energy data from the solar PV system, battery storage, EV charging stations, and grid interactions. It generates reports on energy consumption, solar PV generation, grid utilization, and overall system performance. These reports can be used for energy accounting, billing, and performance evaluation [11].

18.3 Mean Square Error

It is common practice to assess the efficacy of a regression model using the Mean Square Error (MSE) statistic. It calculates the average squared difference between a dataset's actual values and projected values. The MSE is calculated by dividing the entire sample count by the sum of the squared differences.

Mathematically, the MSE is computed as follows:

${MSE} = (1/n) \ast \sum{(y_{i} - \overline{y})}^{2}$ (18.1)

Where:

The dataset contains n samples in total.

The actual value of the i-th sample is represented by yi.

The i-th sample's expected value is represented by the symbol.

A method to gauge the typical magnitude of the model's errors is provided by the MSE. It shows larger errors and is hence sensitive to outliers because of the squared disparities. Better model performance is indicated by a lower MSE score, which implies that the model's predictions are more in line with the actual values [12].

The MSE is frequently used in a variety of fields, including machine learning, statistics, and signal processing, to assess the accuracy and efficiency of regression models. It serves as a benchmark for comparing many models or iterations of the same model to see which one best matches the data [13].

18.4 MSE Significance of EV Charging Stations

18.4.1 Charging Efficiency Evaluation

MSE can be used to assess the accuracy of charging models or algorithms used in EV charging stations. Charging models predict factors such as charging time, energy consumption, or charging power required. By comparing the predicted values with the actual values obtained during charging, MSE can provide a measure of how well the model performs. A lower MSE indicates higher accuracy and efficiency in predicting charging behavior [14].

18.4.2 Load Management Optimization

MSE can be utilized to optimize load management strategies in EV charging stations. Load management involves balancing the charging demand with the available power capacity. By predicting and optimizing charging profiles for multiple EVs, an EMS (Energy Management System) in the charging station can minimize the discrepancy between the predicted and actual charging loads. MSE can be used as an objective function to minimize the difference and improve load management efficiency [15].

18.4.3 Battery State-of-Charge (SoC) Estimation

It is frequently necessary for EV charging stations to precisely determine the batteries' state of charge. MSE can be used to assess the precision of SoC estimate models or algorithms. The accuracy and dependability of the estimating process can be determined by MSE by comparing the estimated SoC values with real measurements. Better SoC estimation performance is shown by lower MSE values [16].

18.4.4 Grid Interaction and Energy Management

EV charging stations can participate in grid-level energy management programs, such as demand response or grid-balancing initiatives. In such cases, the MSE can play a role in assessing the accuracy of the charging station's load prediction and response to grid signals. By minimizing the MSE between the predicted and actual load or power consumption during grid interactions, the charging station can optimize its contribution to grid stability and minimize grid-related costs [17].

MSE serves as a useful metric in EV charging stations for evaluating charging models, optimizing load management, estimating battery SoC, and improving grid interaction strategies. By minimizing the MSE, charging stations can enhance efficiency, accuracy, and reliability, leading to better utilization of resources and improved integration of EVs into the power grid.

18.5 Soft Computing Requirements in EV Charging Station

Soft computing techniques are often required in various applications, including Electric Vehicle (EV) charging stations, due to their ability to handle uncertainty, imprecision, and complex problems that may not be easily solvable by traditional algorithms [18].

18.5.1 Handling Uncertainty

Soft computing techniques, such as fuzzy logic and probabilistic methods, are well-suited for dealing with uncertainty in EV charging scenarios. Factors like user behavior, traffic conditions, and renewable energy availability can introduce uncertainty into the charging process. Soft computing allows for modeling and decision-making under uncertain conditions, enabling more robust and adaptive charging strategies.

18.5.2 Managing Complexity

EV charging stations involve multiple interconnected components, including EVs, batteries, renewable energy sources, and the power grid. Soft computing techniques, such as neural networks and genetic algorithms, can handle the complexity of these systems and assist in optimizing various aspects like load management, charging schedules, and grid integration.

18.5.3 Handling Non-Linearity

The charging behavior of EVs and the relationship between different charging parameters often exhibit nonlinear characteristics. Soft computing techniques excel at modeling and capturing non-linear relationships, allowing for more accurate predictions and control in EV charging stations.

18.5.4 Adaptive Decision Making

Soft computing techniques can adapt and learn from historical data or real-time feedback, enabling the EV charging station to make informed decisions based on changing conditions. These techniques can self-adjust charging strategies based on factors like user preferences, energy availability, grid signals, and environmental conditions.

18.5.5 Optimization and Search

Soft computing techniques, such as evolutionary algorithms and swarm intelligence, are effective in optimization and search problems. They can help find optimal charging schedules, load balancing strategies, and energy management policies in EV charging stations, considering multiple objectives and constraints.

18.5.6 Human-Like Decision Making

Soft computing techniques, particularly fuzzy logic, can emulate human-like decision-making processes by incorporating expert knowledge and linguistic rules. This can be beneficial for handling subjective criteria, user preferences, and accommodating different charging scenarios or policies.

EV charging stations' efficiency, dependability, and adaptability will all increase thanks to the use of soft computing approaches. These techniques allow for the management of complex systems, the making of wise judgments, the handling of uncertainty, the optimization of many elements of EV charging, and finally the integration of electric vehicles and renewable energy sources into the power grid.

18.6 Small-Sized SMES Application in an EV Charging Station with DC Bus and PV System

In this work, the utility and control of a photovoltaic production arrangement with an electric voltage charging of a tiny superconducting magnetic energy storage arrangement is analyzed and the gaps are filled by the proposed system methodology. The management of energy in the electro voltaic chargeable locations is done via transferring the energy through photovoltaic elements, superconducting magnetic energy storage arrangements, electro voltaic batteries and grid. The validation of the proposed energy management system is done via evaluating the system on the basis of efficiency in Simulink of MATLAB package [19]

18.7 The Grid Application of Photovoltaic Charging Stations for Electric Vehicles

The variety and volume of EV batteries connected to the grid keep expanding quickly, placing a significant load on it. Vehicle to Grid (V2G) is a concept which was offered as a solution to this issue to allow electric vehicles to function which serves to be an altered source for charging in the power network. Vehicle to Grid technology has greater potential to dramatically improve the smart grid system's adaptability and storage of sporadic renewable energy sources like wind and solar [20].

Several EV charging station-related studies have been published in the previous five years with the concept of LMA search algorithm and the Grid Partition algorithm based solar PV system for actual economic load dispatch difficulties. In contrast to the almost 31 articles published in Google Scholar, nearly 133 articles were published in IEEE Xplore.

18.8 Proposed AGTO

The majority of metaheuristics were inspired by the collective intelligence of natural species in nature and are crucial for addressing optimization problems. This work suggests the Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimizer (GTO), a new metaheuristic algorithm that draws inspiration from the social intelligence of gorilla troops in the wild. In this program, the gorillas' social structure is mathematically described, and new methods are developed to conduct exploration and exploitation. 52 common benchmark functions and seven technical challenges are used to evaluate the performance of the GTO. The proposed approach, along with a few current metaheuristics, were statistically analyzed using Friedman's test and Wilcoxon rank-sum statistical tests. The outcomes show that, especially for high-dimensional problems, the GTO outperforms comparable algorithms on the majority of benchmark functions. The results demonstrate that, in comparison to other metaheuristics, the GTO can produce superior results [21].

18.9 Neighborhood Nodes Training

Although frequently employed as a neural network training method, the Levenberg Marquardt Algorithm (LMA) has the drawback of being quite costly, since it requires huge storage space for its operation, which in turn increases the epochs to reach the minimum error of the model. Such a hitch in the algorithm hikes the computational complexity of the model since the size of the model is exponentially increasing with the increase in the number of weights to be adjusted. LMA is an optimization algorithm used for solving nonlinear least squares problems.

LMA is particularly effective for minimizing the sum of the squares of the residuals between the observed and predicted values in a nonlinear regression problem. It is commonly used in fields such as curve fitting, data modeling, and optimization. LMA combines the benefits of both the Gauss-Newton algorithm and the steepest descent method. It adjusts the parameters of a nonlinear model by iteratively updating them in a way that reduces the error between the model predictions and the observed data. At each iteration, the algorithm computes the Jacobian matrix, which represents the partial derivatives of the model with respect to each parameter. It then uses this matrix along with the current parameter values to compute an update direction for the parameters.

The update direction is determined by solving a modified linear least squares problem, where a damping factor is introduced to control the step size. LMA continues iteratively until a convergence criterion is met, such as reaching a specified tolerance level or a maximum number of iterations. Upon convergence, it provides an estimate of the optimal parameter values that minimize the sum of squares of residuals. It is an effective optimization technique for solving nonlinear least squares problems. It strikes a balance between the Gauss-Newton method and the steepest descent method, providing a robust and efficient approach for parameter estimation in nonlinear regression and optimization tasks [22].

The AGTO inference system, due to its simplicity in error selection and its mean square error method, achieved better performance. EV charging station for an energy management system using neural networks and achieved better performance in mean square error. The charging station used solar panels and achieved the significant mean square error using LMA neural network. The system promotes the charging performance of the E vehicle using a neural network based self-charging algorithm and achieves better mean square error performance.

In the existing methods the mean square error of the power charging stations is very high and unstable due to the load requirement. So, the proposal system focused on using a Bayesian, LMA neural network in comparison with the AGTO to provide a better mean square error performance of the network.

18.10 Methodology

The number of samples and the G power were calculated using the G power calculator. Utilized G power with 80% for each test and 10 samples for each group. Group 1 includes the Bayesian method, Group 2 the LMA neural network algorithm, and Group 3 the AGTO network algorithm.

18.10.1 Sample 1 Preparation – Bayesian Neural Networks

Bayesian neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of the data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

  1. Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
  2. Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
  3. Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
  4. Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

18.10.2 Sample 2 Preparation – LMA Neural Networks

LMA neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of the data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

  1. Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
  2. Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
  3. Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
  4. Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

18.10.3 Sample 3 Preparation – AGTO Network Algorithm

AGTO neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

  1. Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
  2. Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
  3. Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
  4. Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

The Mean Square Error of the EV charging station was analyzed using the Bayesian method, LMA neural network algorithm, and AGTO network algorithm using the MATLAB R2018a program. The testing setup was carried out utilizing the Intel i5 processor and 4GB RAM of the MATLAB R2018a SIMULINK program. It combines computing and programming in a user-friendly environment where problems and solutions are expressed using recognizable symbols.

18.11 Statistical Analysis

Using an independent sample t-test, the IBM SPSS statistical tool was utilized to analyze the Bayesian, LMA, and AGTO neural networks. For the analysis of independent sample t-tests, the dependent and independent variables are employed. The epoch values at various points are regarded as independent variables, while the mean square error is a dependent variable.

18.12 Result

A comparison of the Bayesian, LMA neural network, and AGTO algorithms' group statistics is implemented. The mean square error (MSE) of the Bayesian neural network is 0.079158. As shown in Table 18.1, the LMA neural network achieved a mean square error (MSE) of 0.055524 and the AGTO achieved a mean square error (MSE) of 0.011789.

18.13 Discussion

In this work, it was concluded that the AGTO has a better mean value and mean square error when compared with the Bayesian neural, LMA neural network by performing an independent t-test. LMA neural network algorithm using PV grid and achieved the mean square error value of 1.008. The energy management uses solar panels to achieve the mean square error of 1.003 using Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm for efficient utilization of available resources. Developed the energy management system in electric vehicles and its components' analysis in various epochs with a better mean square error at 146 epochs. The electric propulsion system and battery management system using Bayesian neural network and achieved the mean square error of -8.043. Developed the battery management system using LMA neural network and swarm optimization method and achieved the mean square error of -9.452. The power allocation approach for load management in electric vehicles using power allocation algorithm and achieved a better mean square error at 565 epoxies.

The limitation in the proposed system is that if the number of electric vehicles (demand) necessitates additional (electric vehicles) EV and subsequently the number of grids needed also increases, resulting in complexity. In future, high-quality substrate and high voltage outcome-based grids can be installed to avoid the complexity.

18.14 Conclusion

An Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimization (AGTO) for minimizing the mean square error of the energy management system in solar PV powered EV charging stations using Levenberg-Marquardt (LMA) Neural Network, Bayesian Neural Network Neural Network is proposed and implemented. The proposed AGTO model gains significantly better quantification results when compared to the LMA and Bayesian network. The generation of around 10,137 kWh energy on an annual basis is sufficiently abundant for charging around 450 vehicle batteries of size 27 kWh.
